Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You're given an array a of length n. You can perform the following operation on it as many times as you want:

  * Pick two integers i and j (1 ≤ i,j ≤ n) such that a_i+a_j is odd, then swap a_i and a_j. 



What is lexicographically the smallest array you can obtain?

An array x is [lexicographically smaller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicographical_order) than an array y if there exists an index i such that x_i<y_i, and x_j=y_j for all 1 ≤ j < i. Less formally, at the first index i in which they differ, x_i<y_i

Input

The first line contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5) — the number of elements in the array a.

The second line contains n space-separated integers a_1, a_2, …, a_{n} (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^9) — the elements of the array a.

Output

The only line contains n space-separated integers, the lexicographically smallest array you can obtain.

Examples

Input


3
4 1 7


Output


1 4 7 

Input


2
1 1


Output


1 1 

Note

In the first example, we can swap 1 and 4 since 1+4=5, which is odd.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n
2. We can swap two elements a_i and a_j if and only if a_i + a_j is odd
3. We want the lexicographically smallest array possible

## Key Insight

For two numbers to have an odd sum, one must be even and one must be odd. This is because:
- even + even = even
- odd + odd = even  
- even + odd = odd

So we can only swap elements between even and odd numbers.

## What Can We Achieve?

Since we can only swap even and odd numbers, we can:
1. Rearrange even numbers among themselves (by swapping through odd numbers)
2. Rearrange odd numbers among themselves (by swapping through even numbers)

But wait - can we actually move even numbers freely? Let me think:

Actually, since we can only swap even with odd, we can't directly swap two evens or two odds. However, we can:
- Take an even number and swap it with an odd number
- Then take that odd number and swap it with another even number, and so on

This means that we can effectively mix even and odd numbers, but we can't change the total count of evens and odds.

However, the key insight is that we can fully rearrange both even and odd positions. Since we can swap any even with any odd (as long as we have at least one of each), we can achieve any permutation of the even numbers in even positions and any permutation of the odd numbers in odd positions.

But actually, let me reconsider. If we have any even and any odd number, we can swap them. This creates a connected component where even and odd numbers can be freely exchanged.

Wait, no: if we have just evens, we can't swap any of them. If we have just odds, we can't swap any of them. But if we have both even and odd, we can swap them.

Since we can perform operations as many times as we want, the key question is: can we achieve any arrangement where we have the same number of even and odd numbers in their respective positions?

Actually, I think we can achieve complete flexibility. Let me think of it this way:

1. All even numbers can be rearranged among themselves (by swapping through odd numbers)
2. All odd numbers can be rearranged among themselves (by swapping through even numbers)

But is this true? Consider: to move an even number from position i to position j, I need to swap it with an odd number at position k, then swap the odd number from position k to position j, and then swap the even number from position j back to position i. This is possible.

Actually, since we can swap any even with any odd (because the sum is odd), we can create a complete mixing. This means that we can achieve any permutation of the even numbers in the even positions and any permutation of the odd numbers in the odd positions?

Wait, no. Actually, we need to think differently.

The operation is symmetric: if I swap a_i and a_j where one is even and one is odd, both positions get the values of the other. This means that even and odd numbers can "flow" between positions.

The key insight is that we can achieve any arrangement where the total number of even and odd numbers remains the same. But actually, we can rearrange the elements arbitrarily as long as we maintain the count of even and odd numbers.

Wait, let me think of a simpler example:
- Array: [4, 1, 7] (even, odd, odd)
- We can swap 4 and 1: [1, 4, 7]
- We can swap 4 and 7: [7, 1, 4]
- We can swap 1 and 7: [4, 7, 1]

So we can get any permutation of the elements! But is that true?

Actually, since we have both even and odd elements, we can really permute all elements freely. 

Wait, let me verify: in the first example, we start with [4,1,7] and want [1,4,7]. We can swap 4 and 1 to get [1,4,7]. That's exactly the answer.

In the second example: [1,1] - both are odd, so no swaps are possible. The answer is [1,1].

So the rule is:
- If we have at least one even and one odd number, we can rearrange all numbers freely to get the lexicographically smallest arrangement.
- If we have all even numbers or all odd numbers, we can only keep the elements in their relative positions to each other.

Actually, if we have all even or all odd, we cannot swap any elements (since even+even=even, odd+odd=even, so no swap is possible).

But if we have at least one even and one odd, we can perform swaps to reach any arrangement.

This means we can achieve the lexicographically smallest array by simply sorting the entire array.

Wait, is this correct? Let me think about a case with [2, 4, 1, 3]:
- We have both evens and odds, so we can permute freely
- The sorted version would be [1, 2, 3, 4]
- Is this achievable? Yes, by appropriate swaps.

In the first example: [4,1,7] → sorted is [1,4,7], which matches.

In the second example: [1,1] → all odd, no swaps possible, so answer is [1,1].

So the algorithm is:
1. If the array contains both even and odd numbers, sort the entire array
2. If the array contains only even numbers or only odd numbers, sort the array (since we can't change the order of elements of the same parity)

Wait, actually if all elements are the same parity, we cannot swap any pairs, so we cannot change the positions. But we can't change positions? 

No, if all elements are odd (or all even), then for any two elements, their sum is even, so we cannot perform any swap. Therefore, the array remains unchanged.

But if we have at least one even and one odd, we can freely rearrange the elements.

Therefore, the solution is:
1. Check if there's at least one even and at least one odd number
2. If yes, sort the entire array
3. If no, return the original array (since we can't make any swaps)

Wait, but if we have mixed parity, can we really sort the array? Let me verify with the first example:
- [4,1,7]: has both even and odd → sort to [1,4,7] ✓

Second example: [1,1]: all odd → no swaps → output [1,1] ✓

Another example: [2,3,4,5] - mixed parity → should sort to [2,3,4,5] which is already sorted.

But wait - can we get [2,3,4,5] from [2,3,4,5]? We can sort it by performing swaps.

Since we can swap any even with any odd, and we have evens (2,4) and odds (3,5), we can perform swaps to achieve any permutation, including the sorted one.

Therefore, the algorithm is:
- Check if there is at least one even and at least one odd number
- If yes, sort the array
- If no, return the array as is

Wait, actually, if the array has only odds or only evens, we cannot perform any swaps, so the array remains unchanged. But if it has both, we can achieve any permutation by swapping through the mixed elements.

Therefore, when we have mixed parity, we can sort the array.

So the final solution:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Check if there's at least one even and one odd number
has_even = any(x % 2 == 0 for x in a)
has_odd = any(x % 2 == 1 for x in a)

if has_even and has_odd:
    # We can perform swaps to get any permutation, so we sort
    a.sort()
else:
    # No swaps possible, array remains unchanged
    pass

print(*a)
```

  * Pick two integers i and j (1 ≤ i,j ≤ n) such that a_i+a_j is odd, then swap a_i and a_j. 



What is lexicographically the smallest array you can obtain?

An array x is [lexicographically smaller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicographical_order) than an array y if there exists an index i such that x_i<y_i, and x_j=y_j for all 1 ≤ j < i. Less formally, at the first index i in which they differ, x_i<y_i

Input

The first line contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5) — the number of elements in the array a.

The second line contains n space-separated integers a_1, a_2, …, a_{n} (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^9) — the elements of the array a.

Output

The only line contains n space-separated integers, the lexicographically smallest array you can obtain.

1 1

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers
2. We can swap two elements a_i and a_j if their sum is odd
3. We want the lexicographically smallest array possible

## Key Insight

For two numbers to have an odd sum: one must be even and one must be odd.
- Even + Odd = Odd
- Even + Even = Even
- Odd + Odd = Even

So we can only swap elements when one is even and one is odd.

## Analysis of What We Can Achieve

Since we can only swap even and odd numbers (not even-even or odd-odd), this means:
- Even numbers can only be swapped with odd numbers
- Even numbers cannot be swapped with other even numbers
- Odd numbers cannot be swapped with other odd numbers

However, when we swap an even and an odd number, we're essentially moving them around. The key insight is that through a series of swaps, we can rearrange the even and odd numbers in any order we want, as long as we maintain the constraint that we can only swap even-odd pairs.

Wait, let me think more carefully:

If I have an even number and an odd number, I can swap them. After swapping, the even number is where the odd number was, and the odd number is where the even number was.

This means that I can move any even number to any position that originally had an odd number, and any odd number to any position that originally had an even number.

But importantly, the total count of even numbers remains the same, and the total count of odd numbers remains the same.

## What Does This Mean for Lexicographically Smallest?

To get the lexicographically smallest array:
- We want the smallest numbers first
- We should try to put the smallest available numbers in the earliest positions

Since we can freely rearrange even and odd numbers (by swapping them with each other), we can arrange the elements optimally.

The constraint is that if we want to put a particular number in a position, we need to ensure we can reach that position through valid swaps.

But here's the crucial observation: since we can swap any even with any odd, we can achieve any permutation of the even numbers among the odd positions, and any permutation of the odd numbers among the even positions.

Therefore, we can achieve the following:
1. Sort all the numbers in ascending order
2. The result will be the sorted array

Wait, is that true? Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [4, 1, 7]
- Even: 4
- Odd: 1, 7
- After sorting: [1, 4, 7]
- This matches the expected output.

Example 2: [1, 1]
- Even: none
- Odd: 1, 1
- After sorting: [1, 1]
- This matches the expected output.

So the key insight is that we can freely rearrange even and odd numbers with each other. This means we can achieve any arrangement where the even numbers are in odd positions and odd numbers are in even positions, but actually we can achieve more - we can arrange all numbers in any order.

Wait, let me reconsider. When we swap an even and odd, we're just exchanging positions. Since we can perform multiple such swaps, and there are no restrictions preventing us from moving any number to any position (through a chain of swaps), we can achieve any permutation of the array.

But is that true? Let's think: can we move an even number to any position?

Yes, because:
- If there's an odd number in a position, we can swap the even number with that odd number
- We can continue this process until the even number reaches its target position
- Similarly, odd numbers can reach any position

Therefore, we can achieve any permutation of the array, as long as we can maintain the option to swap between evens and odds.

Actually, let me reconsider: we have even and odd numbers. We can only swap between even and odd positions. That means:
- If we want to move an even number to a position that currently has an even number, we need to swap it with an odd number first
- But after swapping, the even number moves to the odd position, and the odd number moves to the even position

The key insight is that through a series of swaps, we can achieve any arrangement where the count of even numbers in each position matches the original counts. However, since we can swap any even with any odd, we can achieve the fully sorted array.

This is because we can use the fact that we can "bubble" numbers to their sorted positions through a chain of valid swaps.

Therefore, the lexicographically smallest array we can achieve is simply the sorted array of the original elements.

## Solution

Sort the array in ascending order and return it.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

a.sort()
print(*a)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers
2. We can swap two elements a_i and a_j if and only if a_i + a_j is odd
3. We want the lexicographically smallest array possible

## Key Insight

For two numbers to have an odd sum: one must be even and one must be odd.

So we can only swap elements between even and odd positions, not within the same parity group.

## Analysis

When can we swap elements?
- Even + Odd = Odd (we can swap)
- Even + Even = Even (cannot swap)
- Odd + Odd = Even (cannot swap)

This means:
- Elements of the same parity (all even or all odd) cannot be swapped with each other
- We can only swap even elements with odd elements

## Implications for lexicographical ordering

Since we can only swap between even and odd elements, we cannot change the relative ordering of elements of the same parity.

However, we can rearrange even and odd elements freely as long as they maintain their parity groups.

For lexicographically smallest result:
- We want the smallest elements at the beginning
- But we cannot break the parity constraint

Actually, let me think more carefully:

Therefore, to get the lexicographically smallest array:
- We should assign the smallest available numbers to the earliest positions
- But we need to respect that at each position, we can only place an element that can be "reached" from the original array through valid swaps

- The count of odd elements in odd positions and even positions can vary
- Actually, we can achieve any arrangement where the total number of odd elements and even elements remains unchanged

- The total number of odd elements remains the same
- The total number of even elements remains the same

Therefore, we can sort all the elements and assign them to positions in a way that gives us the lexicographically smallest result.

Wait, that's not quite right. Let me reconsider.

Actually, since we can swap any even with any odd element, we can achieve any permutation of the elements! Is that true?

No - when we swap, we're exchanging positions. But since we can do this repeatedly, we can indeed achieve any permutation of the elements, as long as we can find a sequence of swaps that leads to the desired arrangement.

Actually, think about it: if we have at least one even and one odd number, we can use a sequence of swaps to rearrange any elements. For example, we could "bubble" any element to any position using swaps with other elements.

But let me verify: if we have at least one even and one odd number, we can create a "transport" mechanism. For instance, we can move an even element from position i to position j by swapping it with an odd element at position k, then swap the odd element from k to i, etc.

Therefore, if we have both even and odd numbers, we can achieve any permutation.

What if all elements are even? Then no swaps are possible (since even + even = even), so the array stays the same.

What if all elements are odd? Then no swaps are possible (since odd + odd = even), so the array stays the same.

So the solution is:
1. If all elements are even or all elements are odd, the answer is just the sorted array
2. If there are both even and odd elements, we can rearrange freely, so we sort all elements

Wait, this can't be right. If we have both even and odd elements, can we really achieve any permutation?

Let me think again. When we swap an even and an odd element, we're exchanging their positions. So starting with some arrangement, we can create new arrangements where even and odd elements are in different positions.

But can we get any arrangement? For example, can we move the smallest element to position 0?

Yes, because we have at least one even and one odd element. We can use a "bubble sort" like approach: repeatedly swap the element at position 0 with some other element that has an odd sum with it (i.e., if position 0 has an even element, swap with any odd element, and vice versa).

Since we have both even and odd elements, we can always find a valid swap partner. This means we can move any element to any position through a series of swaps.

Therefore, if there are both even and odd elements, we can achieve any permutation of the elements. So the lexicographically smallest array is just the sorted array.

If all elements are even or all are odd, we cannot perform any swaps, so the array remains unchanged, and the lexicographically smallest is just the sorted array.

Wait, even in the case where all are same parity, the sorted array is still the lexicographically smallest.

So in all cases, the lexicographically smallest array we can achieve is simply the sorted array of the original elements.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [4, 1, 7]
- 4 is even, 1 and 7 are odd
- We can swap 4 and 1 (4+1=5 is odd), or 4 and 7 (4+7=11 is odd)
- After sorting: [1, 4, 7] ✓

Example 2: [1, 1]
- Both are odd
- No swaps possible (1+1=2 is even)
- Already sorted: [1, 1] ✓

So the solution is simply to sort the array.

```python
n = int(input())
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers
2. We can swap two elements a[i] and a[j] if and only if a[i] + a[j] is odd
3. We want to find the lexicographically smallest array possible

## Key Insight

For two numbers to have an odd sum: one must be even and one must be odd (even + odd = odd).

This means we can only swap elements between even and odd positions. We cannot swap two even numbers or two odd numbers.

## What can we achieve?

Since we can only swap even and odd numbers, we need to think about what permutations are possible:

- We can freely rearrange even numbers among themselves? No - we can only swap with odd numbers
- We can freely rearrange odd numbers among themselves? No - we can only swap with even numbers

Actually, let me think more carefully: since we can swap any even number with any odd number, we can essentially "transport" even numbers to odd positions and odd numbers to even positions.

But here's the key insight: **We can achieve any permutation where the count of even numbers and odd numbers remains the same, and we can rearrange them arbitrarily as long as we don't violate the parity constraint.**

Wait, let me reconsider: since we can swap any even with any odd, this creates a fully connected graph between even and odd positions. This means we can achieve any arrangement where the even numbers are scattered among the positions, as long as we maintain the same total count of even and odd numbers.

Actually, the constraint is that we can only swap when the sum is odd, which means we can only swap even with odd. But since we can do this repeatedly, we can effectively move any even number to any odd position and any odd number to any even position.

Therefore, we can achieve any arrangement where the total count of even numbers and odd numbers remains unchanged.

## Goal: Lexicographically smallest

To get the lexicographically smallest array, we want the smallest numbers to appear first.

Since we can rearrange even and odd numbers freely among positions, we can sort all numbers and assign them to positions optimally.

But we need to be careful: we can only swap even with odd, not rearrange within parity groups.

Actually, the key insight is that we can achieve any permutation where the even and odd numbers can be redistributed freely. This means we can sort all the numbers and then assign them to positions.

Wait, let me think with an example:
- [4, 1, 7]: 4 is even, 1 and 7 are odd
- We can swap 4 and 1 → [1, 4, 7]
- We can swap 4 and 7 → [7, 1, 4]
- We can swap 1 and 7 → [4, 7, 1]

So we can get [1, 4, 7], [4, 1, 7], [1, 7, 4], [4, 7, 1], [7, 1, 4], [7, 4, 1]

The lexicographically smallest is [1, 4, 7].

This suggests that we can rearrange the elements freely among positions, as long as we maintain the count of even and odd numbers.

Therefore, the optimal strategy is simply to sort the entire array in ascending order.

Let me verify with the second example:
- [1, 1]: both are odd, so we cannot swap them (1+1=2 is even)
- So we cannot make any changes
- The answer is [1, 1]

This matches.

## Algorithm

1. Read the input array
2. Sort the array in ascending order
3. Output the sorted array

This works because:
- If we have at least one even and one odd number, we can use swaps to move any even number to any position and any odd number to any position
- If all numbers have the same parity (all even or all odd), we cannot make any swaps, so the array stays the same
- In any case, sorting gives us the lexicographically smallest arrangement

Actually, let me think again about the case where all numbers have the same parity. In this case, since we cannot perform any swaps, the array remains unchanged. But if we sort it, we get the lexicographically smallest arrangement.

For example: [2, 4, 6] - all even, cannot swap, so [2, 4, 6] is the answer.
[3, 5, 7] - all odd, cannot swap, so [3, 5, 7] is the answer.

But is [2, 4, 6] really the lexicographically smallest? Yes, because we cannot rearrange it.

So in all cases, sorting gives us the lexicographically smallest possible array.

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Sort the array to get lexicographically smallest
a.sort()

print(' '.join(map(str, a)))
```

What is lexicographically the smallest array you can obtain?

An array x is [lexicographically smaller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicographical_order) than an array y if there exists an index i such that x_i<y_i, and x_j=y_j for all 1 ≤ j < i. Less formally, at the first index i in which they differ, x_i<y_i

Input

Output

The only line contains n space-separated integers, the lexicographically smallest array you can obtain.

1 1

In the non-mixed case, we cannot change the order, so we output the original array.

But is the original array sorted? Not necessarily. 

For example: [3, 1, 5] - all odd, no swaps, output is [3, 1, 5].

But let me double-check the problem statement: "What is lexicographically the smallest array you can obtain?"

In the non-mixed case, we can only obtain the original array, so that's what we get.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Check if there is at least one even and one odd number
has_even = any(x % 2 == 0 for x in a)
has_odd = any(x % 2 == 1 for x in a)

if has_even and has_odd:
    # We can achieve any permutation, so output sorted array
    print(' '.join(map(str, sorted(a))))
else:
    # Cannot perform any swaps, so output original array
    print(' '.join(map(str, a)))
```

Wait, this doesn't seem right. In the non-mixed case, we cannot sort the array. But the problem might be expecting us to sort it in all cases.

Let me reconsider: is there any way to achieve a sorted array when we have all even or all odd?

No, because we cannot perform any swaps, so the relative order of elements cannot change.

Therefore, we cannot achieve a sorted array in the non-mixed case.

But the example [1, 1] works because it's already sorted.

So I think my reasoning is correct.

However, let me verify with a thought experiment:

Can we ever achieve a sorted array when all elements have the same parity?

No, because no swaps are possible, so the relative order remains unchanged.

Therefore, the only arrangement possible is the original one.

Thus, the final code is:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

has_even = any(x % 2 == 0 for x in a)
has_odd = any(x % 2 == 1 for x in a)

if has_even and has_odd:
    print(' '.join(map(str, sorted(a))))
else:
    print(' '.join(map(str, a)))
```

But wait, what about the case where we have both even and odd - can we really achieve any permutation?

I believe yes. With a series of swaps between even and odd elements, we can generate any permutation. This is a well-known property of swap systems with specific constraints.

Since the operation allows us to swap between any even and any odd element, we have connectivity among all positions, so we can achieve any permutation.

Therefore, the above solution should be correct.

Let me run through the examples:

Example 1: [4, 1, 7] - has both even (4) and odd (1,7), so sort → [1, 4, 7] ✓
Example 2: [1, 1] - all odd, no even, so output original [1, 1] ✓

Another example: [2, 4, 6] - all even, no odd, so output [2, 4, 6]
Another example: [3, 5, 7] - all odd, no even, so output [3, 5, 7]
Another example: [2, 3, 4] - mixed, so sort → [2, 3, 4]

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You're given an array a of length n. You can perform the following operation on it as many times as you want:

  * Pick two integers i and j (1 ≤ i,j ≤ n) such that a_i+a_j is odd, then swap a_i and a_j. 



What is lexicographically the smallest array you can obtain?

An array x is [lexicographically smaller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicographical_order) than an array y if there exists an index i such that x_i<y_i, and x_j=y_j for all 1 ≤ j < i. Less formally, at the first index i in which they differ, x_i<y_i

Input

The first line contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5) — the number of elements in the array a.

The second line contains n space-separated integers a_1, a_2, …, a_{n} (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^9) — the elements of the array a.

Output

The only line contains n space-separated integers, the lexicographically smallest array you can obtain.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers
2. We can swap two elements a_i and a_j if and only if a_i + a_j is odd
3. We want the lexicographically smallest array possible

## Key Insight

For two numbers to have an odd sum: one must be even and one must be odd.
- Even + Odd = Odd
- Even + Even = Even
- Odd + Odd = Even

So we can only swap elements when one is even and one is odd.

## What does this mean for our operations?

Since we can only swap even and odd numbers, we need to think about what permutations are possible:

1. We can swap any even number with any odd number
2. We cannot swap two even numbers or two odd numbers directly
3. However, through a series of swaps, we can move even numbers to positions where odd numbers are, and vice versa

## Can we achieve any arrangement?

Let me think about the constraints:
- We can move any even number to any position, as long as we can pair it with an odd number
- We can move any odd number to any position, as long as we can pair it with an even number

Since we can perform operations as many times as we want, and we can form chains of swaps (e.g., even ↔ odd ↔ even), we can actually rearrange the even and odd numbers in any order we want.

Here's why: if we have an even number at position i and want to move it to position j, we can:
1. Pair it with an odd number at some position k to swap them
2. The even number is now at position k
3. The odd number is now at position i
4. We can repeat this process to move the even number to position j

The key insight is that we can freely rearrange even and odd numbers between each other. We just need to maintain the count of even and odd numbers.

## What is the lexicographically smallest arrangement?

To get the lexicographically smallest array:
- We want smaller numbers to come first
- Since we can rearrange even and odd numbers freely, we should sort all elements
- Then the result will be the sorted array

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

Example 1: [4, 1, 7]
- Even: [4], Odd: [1, 7]
- Sorted: [1, 4, 7] ✓ This matches expected output

Example 2: [1, 1]
- Both are odd
- We cannot swap any two elements (odd + odd = even)
- So we can't change the array
- Answer: [1, 1] ✓ This matches expected output

So when we have only odd numbers, we can't swap any of them, so the array remains unchanged.

When we have both even and odd numbers, we can rearrange them freely.

Therefore, the algorithm is:
1. If all numbers are odd (or we have no even numbers), just sort the array
2. If we have both even and odd numbers, we can freely rearrange them, so sort the entire array

Actually, let me reconsider: even if we have only one even number, we can still swap it with any odd number. Since we can do this repeatedly, we can move the even number to any position by swapping it with odd numbers in sequence.

So in fact, whenever we have at least one even and one odd number, we can achieve any permutation of the elements (since we can use the even-odd swaps to effectively "bubble" elements around).

But wait - is that true? Can we really achieve any permutation?

Actually, since we can swap any even with any odd, this creates a connected component for all even and odd positions. That means we can rearrange the elements in any order we want.

Therefore, regardless of the original arrangement, if there's at least one even and one odd number, we can achieve the sorted array.

If all numbers are odd, we cannot swap any pair, so the array remains unchanged.

## Final Algorithm

1. Read the array
2. If all elements are odd, output the original array (already sorted, but we need to sort it anyway since it's already the smallest)
3. Otherwise, sort the entire array and output it

Actually, even when all are odd, sorting gives the lexicographically smallest arrangement.

So the solution is simply: **sort the array**

Let me verify:
- Example 1: [4,1,7] → sorted: [1,4,7] ✓
- Example 2: [1,1] → sorted: [1,1] ✓

This makes sense because:
- When all elements are odd: no swaps possible, so sorted array is best
- When we have at least one even: we can perform swaps to achieve any arrangement, so sorted array is achievable and lexicographically smallest

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Sort the array to get lexicographically smallest arrangement
a.sort()
print(*a)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n
2. We can swap two elements a_i and a_j if and only if a_i + a_j is odd
3. We want to find the lexicographically smallest array possible

## Key Insight

For two numbers to have an odd sum: one must be even and one must be odd.

So we can only swap elements between even and odd positions. We cannot swap two even numbers or two odd numbers because their sum would be even.

## Analysis

Since we can only swap even and odd numbers, the relative positions of even numbers and odd numbers cannot change. We can only rearrange even numbers among themselves and odd numbers among themselves, but we can "transmit" even numbers to odd positions and vice versa.

Wait, let me rethink this. If we have an even number and an odd number, we can swap them. This means:
- Even numbers can move to odd positions
- Odd numbers can move to even positions

But crucially, after swapping, the even/odd property is preserved in the sense that the total count of even and odd numbers remains the same.

The key insight is that we can achieve any permutation of the even numbers among the even positions, and any permutation of the odd numbers among the odd positions? No, that's not quite right.

Actually, since we can swap any even with any odd, we can essentially "mix" them freely. What's the constraint?

Let me think differently: if I have an even number and an odd number, I can swap them. After swapping, the even number is in the odd position and the odd number is in the even position.

This means that any even number can potentially move to any position, and any odd number can potentially move to any position, as long as I can find a path to move them there.

But since I can chain swaps, I can actually move any even number to any position, and any odd number to any position. The only constraint is that I need to maintain the overall even/odd count.

However, for the lexicographically smallest array, I want small numbers to appear at the beginning.

## Key Realization

Since we can swap any even with any odd, we can rearrange the entire array in any way we want, as long as the count of even and odd numbers remains the same. This is because:

1. If I want to put a small number in position 0, I can find a path to bring it there
2. The operation allows me to swap any even with any odd, so I can effectively permute the elements freely

Wait, let me verify with the example:
- [4, 1, 7]: 4 is even, 1 and 7 are odd
- 4 + 1 = 5 (odd) ✓, so we can swap 4 and 1
- Result: [1, 4, 7]

This gives us 1 at the beginning, which is better.

Can we get [1, 7, 4]? That would require swapping 4 and 7: 4 + 7 = 11 (odd) ✓, so we can do it.

So we can achieve any arrangement where we have the odd numbers and even numbers in mixed positions.

But what about the count? In the original array, we have 1 even and 2 odd numbers. In [1, 7, 4], we still have 1 even and 2 odd numbers.

So the constraint is that the number of even and odd elements remains unchanged.

Since we want lexicographically smallest, we should sort all elements and then assign them to positions based on what's possible.

But here's the key: we can actually achieve any permutation of the array elements, because we can generate any even-odd pair and swap them. 

For example, to move a specific element to a specific position:
- If it's an even number and we want it in an odd position, we can swap it with any odd number
- If it's an odd number and we want it in an even position, we can swap it with any even number

Since we can perform swaps freely (by chaining), we can achieve any permutation.

Therefore, the lexicographically smallest array is simply the sorted array.

Let me verify with the examples:
- Example 1: [4, 1, 7] → sorted: [1, 4, 7] ✓
- Example 2: [1, 1] → both are odd, so 1+1=2 (even), we cannot perform any swap. So the array remains [1, 1] ✓

Wait, in example 2, both are odd, so we cannot perform any swaps at all. So the answer is just the sorted array.

This holds for all cases: if we can perform swaps, we can achieve any permutation, and if we cannot (when all elements have the same parity), then the array remains unchanged, which is already sorted.

Therefore, the solution is simply to sort the array.

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

a.sort()
print(*a)
```

This works because:
1. If all elements have the same parity (all even or all odd), no swaps are possible, and the sorted array is the best we can do.
2. If there are both even and odd elements, we can perform swaps between even and odd elements, and through a series of such swaps, we can achieve any permutation of the elements.
3. The lexicographically smallest permutation is the sorted array.

The answer is simply the sorted array.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.


-----Examples-----
Input
1 2
DI

Output
Poor Dima!

Input
2 2
MA
ID

Output
Poor Inna!

Input
5 5
DIMAD
DIMAI
DIMAM
DDMAA
AAMID

Output
4



-----Note-----

Notes to the samples:

In the first test sample, Inna cannot go through name DIMA a single time.

In the second test sample, Inna can go through the infinite number of words DIMA. For that, she should move in the clockwise direction starting from the lower right corner.

In the third test sample the best strategy is to start from the cell in the upper left corner of the table. Starting from this cell, Inna can go through name DIMA four times.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to go through the name "DIMA" as many times as possible in a cycle
3. The path must follow: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. Movements are only to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of times she can complete the DIMA cycle

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a graph problem where each cell is a node and edges exist between adjacent cells with consecutive letters in "DIMA"
2. We need to find cycles in this graph
3. If there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely, we output "Poor Inna!"
4. If there's no valid path at all, we output "Poor Dima!"
5. Otherwise, we find the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles

## Approach

1. Build a graph where each position can transition to adjacent positions that form the sequence D→I→M→A→D
2. Use DFS/BFS from each starting D cell to find all reachable paths
3. For each path, calculate how many complete DIMA cycles we can form
4. Check if any path contains a cycle (loop back to a previous state)

However, since the transitions are deterministic (D→I→M→A→D), we can use state compression where the state is (row, col, position in cycle).

The state should be (row, col, step) where step represents which letter we're at in the cycle (0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A).

## Algorithm Steps

1. For each cell with 'D', do DFS/BFS to explore all possible paths
2. Keep track of visited states (row, col, step) to detect cycles
3. If we encounter a state we've seen before, we have an infinite cycle
4. Otherwise, calculate the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles we can make

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all starting positions with 'D'
    start_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                start_positions.append((i, j))
    
    # If no starting position with 'D', can't complete even one cycle
    if not start_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # For each starting position, find the maximum cycles we can achieve
    for start_row, start_col in start_positions:
        # State: (row, col, step) where step = 0,1,2,3 for D,I,M,A
        # We track visited states to detect cycles
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([(start_row, start_col, 0)])  # (row, col, step)
        visited.add((start_row, start_col, 0))
        
        current_cycle_count = 0
        found_infinite = False
        
        # We'll use DFS with backtracking to find maximum cycles
        # But we need to be careful about infinite loops
        
        # Instead, let's do: for each path, calculate how many complete cycles we can make
        # and check if any path contains a cycle
        
        # Use DFS with memoization
        def dfs(row, col, step, path_length):
            nonlocal found_infinite, max_cycles
            
            # If we're at a position we've seen before in this path,
            # we have a cycle (infinite possibility)
            state = (row, col, step)
            if state in visited:
                found_infinite = True
                return 0
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            # Check if we can make a complete DIMA cycle
            next_step = (step + 1) % 4
            next_char = "DIMA"[next_step]
            
            # If next char doesn't exist in adjacent cells, we can't continue
            next_positions = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m and grid[new_row][new_col] == next_char:
                    next_positions.append((new_row, new_col))
            
            # If no valid next position, we can't continue
            if not next_positions:
                # But we might have already completed some cycles
                # If step == 3 (we just finished a DIMA cycle), increment count
                if step == 3:
                    return 1  # We completed one full cycle
                else:
                    return 0
            
            # Otherwise, we continue
            result = 0
            for next_row, next_col in next_positions:
                result += dfs(next_row, next_col, next_step, path_length + 1)
            
            # Remove from visited after processing (backtrack)
            visited.remove(state)
            return result
        
        # But the above DFS will not work well for maximum cycles
        
        # Let's instead use a different approach: 
        # From each starting point, we can calculate the maximum number of complete cycles
        # We need to detect infinite cycles (cycles that can be traversed infinitely)
        
        # For each state, we track if we can reach a cycle or not
        # and the maximum cycles we can make
        
        # Let's do BFS with state (r, c, step) and track the cycle count
        # Actually, we need to find if there's a cycle in the path
        # If we can return to a previous state, we have infinite cycles
        
        state_visited = set()
        queue = deque([(start_row, start_col, 0)])
        state_visited.add((start_row, start_col, 0))
        
        # For each state, we track the max cycles we can achieve from that state
        # But this is complex
        
        # Simpler approach: 
        # 1. Check if any path leads to a cycle (returns to previous state)
        # 2. If yes, output "Poor Inna!"
        # 3. Otherwise, find maximum cycles
        
        # Use DFS to find if there's a cycle
        def has_cycle(r, c, step, visited_set):
            if (r, c, step) in visited_set:
                return True
                
            visited_set.add((r, c, step))
            
            next_step = (step + 1) % 4
            next_char = "DIMA"[next_step]
            
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                    # Check if this leads to a cycle
                    if has_cycle(nr, nc, next_step, visited_set):
                        return True
            
            visited_set.remove((r, c, step))
            return False
        
        # Check if there's any infinite cycle from this start
        if has_cycle(start_row, start_col, 0, set()):
            print("Poor Inna!")
            return
        
        # If no infinite cycle, calculate maximum cycles from this start
        # Use BFS to find the maximum path length in terms of complete DIMA cycles
        
        # We'll do BFS but we care about complete cycles
        # Each complete cycle is 4 steps: D→I→M→A
        # After A, we go back to D
        
        # We want to maximize the number of complete cycles
        # State: (r, c, step) where step in {0,1,2,3}
        
        # Instead of tracking maximum cycles, let's calculate the maximum depth
        # We can have at most some number of cycles before getting stuck
        
        # Use BFS to find the maximum path length
        queue = deque([(start_row, start_col, 0)])
        max_cycle_count = 0
        cycles = 0
        
        # visited states to avoid infinite loops (but we already checked for infinity)
        state_visited = set()
        state_visited.add((start_row, start_col, 0))
        
        while queue:
            r, c, step = queue.popleft()
            
            # If we're at step 3 (A), we've completed one DIMA cycle
            if step == 3:
                cycles += 1
                # After A, we go back to D (step 0)
                next_step = 0
                next_char = "D"
                # Check adjacent cells with 'D'
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        new_state = (nr, nc, next_step)
                        if new_state not in state_visited:
                            state_visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append((nr, nc, next_step))
            else:
                # Move to next step
                next_step = (step + 1) % 4
                next_char = "DIMA"[next_step]
                
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                        new_state = (nr, nc, next_step)
                        if new_state not in state_visited:
                            state_visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append((nr, nc, next_step))
        
        max_cycles = max(max_cycles, cycles)
    
    # If we found any infinite cycle, we already printed "Poor Inna!"
    # Otherwise, if max_cycles is 0, no complete cycle was possible
    if max_cycles == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)
```

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all starting positions with 'D'
    start_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                start_positions.append((i, j))
    
    # If no starting position with 'D', can't complete even one cycle
    if not start_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # Check for infinite cycles and find maximum cycles
    infinite_found = False
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # For each starting position, do BFS to detect cycles and count max cycles
    for start_row, start_col in start_positions:
        # State: (row, col, step) where step = 0,1,2,3 for D,I,M,A
        # We use visited set to detect cycles
        visited = set()
        
        # BFS to explore all reachable states
        queue = deque([(start_row, start_col, 0)])
        visited.add((start_row, start_col, 0))
        
        # Track the maximum number of complete cycles we can achieve
        # Each complete cycle is D->I->M->A, then back to D
        current_cycle_count = 0
        
        # We need to check if we can return to a previous state - that would be a cycle
        # But we're interested in infinite cycles that can be repeated
        while queue:
            r, c, step = queue.popleft()
            
            # Check if this state can lead to a cycle
            # From current state, we go to next letter
            next_step = (step + 1) % 4
            next_char = "DIMA"[next_step]
            
            # Check all adjacent cells with next_char
            next_positions = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                    next_positions.append((nr, nc))
            
            # If there are next positions, we can continue
            if next_positions:
                # For each next position, check if we can form a cycle
                # But we already have visited set - if we can return to a previous state, we have infinite loop
                for nr, nc in next_positions:
                    next_state = (nr, nc, next_step)
                    if next_state in visited:
                        # We found a cycle! This path can be repeated infinitely
                        infinite_found = True
                        break
                    visited.add(next_state)
                    queue.append((nr, nc, next_step))
                else:
                    # No break, so continue with remaining positions in queue
                    pass
            else:
                # No next position - we can't continue
                if step == 3:  # We finished a DIMA cycle
                    current_cycle_count += 1
        
        # Update maximum cycles globally
        max_cycles = max(max_cycles, current_cycle_count)
    
    # If infinite cycle found, output "Poor Inna!"
    if infinite_found:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_cycles == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)
```

Wait, there's still an issue. In the BFS, when we find a next state that's already visited, we just break, but we need to check if we can form a cycle from that state. Actually, if from a state we can reach a previously visited state, that implies we can loop infinitely.

Let me fix the logic:

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                start_positions.append((i, j))

    if not start_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    infinite_found = False
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # For each starting position, do BFS to detect cycles and count max cycles
    for start_row, start_col in start_positions:
        # State: (row, col, step) where step = 0,1,2,3 for D,I,M,A
        visited = set()
        
        # BFS to explore all reachable states
        queue = deque([(start_row, start_col, 0)])
        visited.add((start_row, start_col, 0))
        
        # Track the maximum number of complete cycles we can achieve
        # Each complete cycle is D->I->M->A, then back to D
        current_cycle_count = 0
        
        # Use a dictionary to track how many cycles we can achieve from each state
        # But simpler: just check if we can return to a visited state
        
        while queue:
            r, c, step = queue.popleft()
            
            # Check if we can form a full DIMA cycle (D->I->M->A)
            # The next step is (step + 1) % 4
            next_step = (step + 1) % 4
            next_char = "DIMA"[next_step]
            
            # Find next positions
            next_positions = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                    next_positions.append((nr, nc))
            
            # If there are next positions, check if we can continue
            for nr, nc in next_positions:
                next_state = (nr, nc, next_step)
                
                # If we can reach a state we've visited before, we have a cycle
                if next_state in visited:
                    infinite_found = True
                    break
                    
                # Add to queue and mark as visited
                visited.add(next_state)
                queue.append((nr, nc, next_step))

                # Check if we're at step 3 (A) - we've completed one DIMA cycle
                if step == 3:
                    current_cycle_count += 1
```

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating the cycle detection. When we can go from state S to state T, and T is a state we've already visited, that means we can loop from S to T and back, creating an infinite cycle.

But in our BFS, when we expand a state, we add its neighbors to the queue. If a neighbor state is already in visited, that means we can reach it from multiple paths, which suggests a cycle exists.

However, we need to be careful: if we go A->D->I->M->A, and we've already been to A before from a different path, that creates a cycle.

The logic is correct: if at any point we find that a next state is already visited, then we have a cycle.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 2: 2x2
MA
ID
We have D at (1,1), then I at (1,0), then M at (0,0), then A at (0,1), then D at (1,1) - which is back to start. This should create an infinite cycle.

In our BFS:
- Start at (1,1,0) - D
- Go to (1,0,1) - I
- Go to (0,0,2) - M  
- Go to (0,1,3) - A
- From (0,1,3), next step is 0, look for D. (1,1) has D.
- State (1,1,0) is already visited → cycle found!

Yes, this works.

```python
from collections import deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())
grid = []
for _ in range(n):
    grid.append(input().strip())

# Directions: up, down, left, right
directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

# Find all starting positions with 'D'
start_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if grid[i][j] == 'D':
            start_positions.append((i, j))

# If no starting position with 'D', can't complete even one cycle
if not start_positions:
    print("Poor Dima!")
else:
    infinite_found = False
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # For each starting position, do BFS to detect cycles and count max cycles
    for start_row, start_col in start_positions:
        # State: (row, col, step) where step = 0,1,2,3 for D,I,M,A
        visited = set()
        
        # BFS to explore all reachable states
        queue = deque([(start_row, start_col, 0)])
        visited.add((start_row, start_col, 0))
        
        # Track the maximum number of complete cycles we can achieve
        current_cycle_count = 0
        
        # Use a flag to break if we find an infinite cycle
        while queue:
            r, c, step = queue.popleft()
            
            # Check if we can form a next move
            next_step = (step + 1) % 4
            next_char = "DIMA"[next_step]
            
            # Find next positions with next_char
            next_positions = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                    next_positions.append((nr, nc))
            
            # If there are next positions, process them
            for nr, nc in next_positions:
                next_state = (nr, nc, next_step)
                
                # If we can reach a state we've already visited, we have a cycle
                if next_state in visited:
                    infinite_found = True
                    break
                    
                # Add to queue and mark as visited
                visited.add(next_state)
                queue.append((nr, nc, next_step))
            else:
                # No break, so continue processing queue
                # Check if we're at A (step 3) - we've completed one DIMA cycle
                if step == 3:
                    current_cycle_count += 1
            # Break the inner for loop if we found infinite cycle
            if infinite_found:
                break
        
        # Update maximum cycles globally
        max_cycles = max(max_cycles, current_cycle_count)
    
    # If infinite cycle found, output "Poor Inna!"
    if infinite_found:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_cycles == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.

DIMAD

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to go through the sequence "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. The movement pattern is: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. She can only move to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of times she can go through "DIMA"

## Key Insights

1. **Infinite cycles**: If there's a cycle that allows her to go D→I→M→A→D, she can go through the sequence infinitely
2. **Finite cycles**: From a starting position, she might go through DIMA k times before getting stuck
3. **No path**: If she can't even find one complete DIMA sequence, answer is "Poor Dima!"

## Approach

This is essentially a graph traversal problem where:
- Each cell is a node
- There are directed edges only between cells that form the DIMA sequence
- We need to find if there's a cycle (infinite loop) or what's the maximum finite path

The key insight is that we have a directed cycle: D→I→M→A→D

I can use DFS with cycle detection. If I can reach a state where I'm back at a D cell that I've seen before, that means an infinite loop exists.

However, we need to be careful about the state. The state depends on the current letter and the path taken.

Actually, since we always follow D→I→M→A→D→I→M→A... pattern, we can think of it as having states: D, I, M, A.

For each cell containing letter L, we can only transition to adjacent cells with the next letter in the sequence (D→I, I→M, M→A, A→D).

## Algorithm

1. Create a graph where each cell is a node, and there are edges according to the DIMA sequence
2. For each starting position with 'D', try to find the maximum path length
3. Use DFS with memoization to avoid recomputation
4. Detect cycles - if we visit the same state (position and current phase) twice, we have an infinite loop
5. The state is (row, col, phase) where phase = 0(D), 1(I), 2(M), 3(A)

Wait, let me reconsider. Since the sequence is fixed, from any D, we go to I, from I to M, from M to A, from A to D.

So we can model this as a graph with states being the current position and what phase we're in.

For each starting D cell, we can:
- Perform DFS/BFS to find maximum path length
- Keep track of visited states (row, col, phase) to detect cycles

## Implementation Steps

1. Parse input
2. For each D cell, try to follow the DIMA path
3. Use DFS with memoization to track state (row, col, phase)
4. If we visit a state twice, return infinite
5. Otherwise, return the maximum path length found

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_cycles = 0
    has_infinite = False
    
    # For each starting D position, try to find maximum cycles
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # We'll use DFS with memoization to track visited states
        # State: (row, col, phase) where phase 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, phase, current_cycles):
            nonlocal has_infinite, max_cycles
            
            # State (r, c, phase)
            state = (r, c, phase)
            if state in visited:
                # We've seen this state before - we have a cycle
                has_infinite = True
                return float('inf')  # infinite cycles
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            # If we're at the end of a DIMA sequence (after A), we go back to D
            if phase == 3:  # We just finished A, so next move should be to D
                # Check which adjacent cells have D
                next_phases = []
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        next_phases.append((nr, nc))
                
                if not next_phases:
                    # Can't continue, return current cycles
                    return current_cycles
                
                # Try all possible next D positions
                max_next_cycles = current_cycles
                for nr, nc in next_phases:
                    next_cycles = dfs(nr, nc, 0, current_cycles + 1)
                    if next_cycles == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                    max_next_cycles = max(max_next_cycles, next_cycles)
                
                return max_next_cycles
            else:
                # We're at D (phase 0), I (phase 1), M (phase 2) - we need to go to next letter
                next_phase = phase + 1
                next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
                
                # Find adjacent cells with next_letter
                next_positions = []
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        next_positions.append((nr, nc))
                
                if not next_positions:
                    # Can't continue from this position - return current cycles
                    return current_cycles
                
                # Try all possible next positions
                max_next_cycles = current_cycles
                for nr, nc in next_positions:
                    next_cycles = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase, current_cycles)
                    if next_cycles == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                    max_next_cycles = max(max_next_cycles, next_cycles)
                
                return max_next_cycles
        
        # Start DFS from initial position
        result = dfs(start_r, start_c, 0, 0)
        
        if result == float('inf'):
            has_infinite = True
        else:
            max_cycles = max(max_cycles, result)
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)

solve()
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_cycles = 0
    has_infinite = False
    
    # For each starting D position, try to find maximum cycles
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # We'll use DFS with memoization to track visited states
        # State: (row, col, phase) where phase 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, phase, cycles):
            nonlocal has_infinite, max_cycles
            
            state = (r, c, phase)
            if state in visited:
                # We've seen this state before - we have a cycle
                has_infinite = True
                return float('inf')  # infinite cycles
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            # Determine next phase based on current phase
            # From D(0) -> I(1), I(1) -> M(2), M(2) -> A(3), A(3) -> D(0)
            next_phase = phase + 1
            if next_phase == 4:
                next_phase = 0
            
            # Next letter in sequence
            next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
            
            # Find adjacent cells with next_letter
            next_positions = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    next_positions.append((nr, nc))
            
            if not next_positions:
                # No valid next move - we can't continue this path
                return cycles
            
            # Try all possible next positions
            max_next_cycles = cycles
            for nr, nc in next_positions:
                next_cycles = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase, cycles + 1)
                if next_cycles == float('inf'):
                    return float('inf')
                max_next_cycles = max(max_next_cycles, next_cycles)
            
            return max_next_cycles
        
        # Start DFS from initial position
        result = dfs(start_r, start_c, 0, 0)
        
        if result == float('inf'):
            has_infinite = True
        else:
            max_cycles = max(max_cycles, result)
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)
```

from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_cycles = 0
    has_infinite = False
    
    # For each starting D position, try to find maximum cycles
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # We'll use DFS with memoization to track visited states
        # State: (row, col, phase) where phase 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, phase, cycles):
            nonlocal has_infinite, max_cycles
            
            state = (r, c, phase)
            if state in visited:
                # We've seen this state before - we have a cycle
                has_infinite = True
                return float('inf')  # infinite cycles
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            # Determine next phase based on current phase
            # From D(0) -> I(1), I(1) -> M(2), M(2) -> A(3), A(3) -> D(0)
            next_phase = phase + 1
            if next_phase == 4:
                next_phase = 0
            
            # Next letter in sequence
            next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
            
            # Find adjacent cells with next_letter
            next_positions = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    next_positions.append((nr, nc))
            
            if not next_positions:
                # No valid next move - we can't continue this path
                return cycles
            
            # Try all possible next positions
            max_next_cycles = cycles
            for nr, nc in next_positions:
                next_cycles = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase, cycles + 1)
                if next_cycles == float('inf'):
                    return float('inf')
                max_next_cycles = max(max_next_cycles, next_cycles)
            
            return max_next_cycles
        
        # Start DFS from initial position
        result = dfs(start_r, start_c, 0, 0)
        
        if result == float('inf'):
            has_infinite = True
        else:
            max_cycles = max(max_cycles, result)
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.
