View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to form the name "DIMA" as many times as possible in sequence
3. The path follows: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. We can only move to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of times "DIMA" can be formed
6. Three cases:
   - Can't form "DIMA" once → "Poor Dima!"
   - Can form infinitely many times → "Poor Inna!"
   - Can form a finite number of times → that number

## Key Insights

1. **Infinite cycles**: If there's a cycle that contains all four letters D, I, M, A in order, we can loop forever. This happens when we can form DIMA and then return to a D via a path that eventually connects back.

2. **Finite paths**: If we can only complete a finite number of DIMA sequences, we need to find the longest path.

3. **No path**: If we can't start with D or can't complete D→I→M→A, then we can't form "DIMA" once.

## Solution Strategy

This is essentially a graph problem where:
- Each cell is a node
- There are directed edges only from D→I, I→M, M→A, A→D
- We need to find the longest path that follows the pattern D→I→M→A→D→...

The key insight is that if there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely, we'll detect it. This happens when we can go from D→I→M→A and then somehow get back to a D through an edge that forms a cycle.

Algorithm:
1. For each starting D cell, try to find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences we can form
2. Use DFS/BFS with state (current_row, current_col, current_phase) where phase indicates which letter we're at in the sequence
3. To detect infinite cycles, we need to track visited states. If we revisit the same state, we have an infinite loop
4. For finite cases, we track the maximum count

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    # If no D found, can't start
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # For each starting D position, find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
    max_count = 0
    has_infinite_cycle = False
    
    # We'll use DFS with memoization to avoid infinite loops
    # State: (row, col, phase) where phase = 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
    # We want to find the maximum count of complete DIMA sequences
    
    def dfs(row, col, phase, visited_states):
        # phase: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # Returns the maximum count of complete DIMA sequences from this state
        
        # If we've seen this state before, we have a cycle
        state = (row, col, phase)
        if state in visited_states:
            return float('inf')  # Infinite cycle detected
        
        visited_states.add(state)
        
        # Find next valid move
        next_phase = phase + 1
        if next_phase == 4:  # We completed one DIMA cycle
            next_phase = 0
            count = 1
            # Look for next D to continue
            new_moves = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m and grid[new_row][new_col] == 'D':
                    new_moves.append((new_row, new_col))
            
            if new_moves:
                # We can continue the cycle
                max_additional = 0
                for nr, nc in new_moves:
                    additional = dfs(nr, nc, 0, visited_states.copy())
                    max_additional = max(max_additional, additional)
                return count + max_additional
            else:
                return count
        
        # Otherwise, we need to move to next letter in sequence
        next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
        
        # Find adjacent cells with the next letter
        next_moves = []
        for dr, dc in directions:
            new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
            if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m and grid[new_row][new_col] == next_letter:
                next_moves.append((new_row, new_col))
        
        if not next_moves:
            # Can't proceed further
            return 0
        
        # Try all possible next moves
        max_next = 0
        for nr, nc in next_moves:
            additional = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase, visited_states.copy())
            max_next = max(max_next, additional)
        
        return max_next
    
    # Try each starting D position
    for d_row, d_col in d_positions:
        # Check if we can even start
        # From D, we need to find I
        visited_states = set()
        result = dfs(d_row, d_col, 0, set())
        
        if result == float('inf'):
            has_infinite_cycle = True
            break
        else:
            max_count = max(max_count, result)
    
    if has_infinite_cycle:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_count == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_count)
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    # If no D found, can't start
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # For each starting D position, we'll try to find the maximum complete DIMA sequences
    # Use DFS with memoization to detect cycles
    # State: (row, col, phase) where phase = 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
    # We'll track the maximum count we can achieve from each state
    
    # Alternative approach: for each starting D, compute the maximum sequences
    # We can use DFS with backtracking and detect cycles
    
    def get_max_sequences(start_row, start_col):
        # Returns maximum sequences we can form starting from (start_row, start_col)
        # We'll use DFS with state (row, col, phase)
        
        # Use memoization to avoid recomputation
        memo = {}
        
        def dfs(row, col, phase):
            state = (row, col, phase)
            if state in memo:
                return memo[state]
            
            # Find next letter in sequence
            next_phase = phase + 1
            if next_phase == 4:
                # We completed one DIMA cycle
                next_phase = 0
                # Look for next D to continue
                next_d_positions = []
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        next_d_positions.append((nr, nc))
                
                # If we can find a D to continue, we can potentially continue
                if next_d_positions:
                    # Try each possible next D
                    max_from_next = 0
                    for nr, nc in next_d_positions:
                        result = dfs(nr, nc, 0)
                        max_from_next = max(max_from_next, result)
                    result = 1 + max_from_next
                else:
                    result = 1
            else:
                # We need to move to the next letter
                next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
                next_positions = []
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        next_positions.append((nr, nc))
                
                if not next_positions:
                    result = 0
                else:
                    # Try each possible next position
                    max_from_next = 0
                    for nr, nc in next_positions:
                        result = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase)
                        max_from_next = max(max_from_next, result)
                    result = max_from_next
            
            memo[state] = result
            return result
        
        # Start from the given position with phase 0 (D)
        return dfs(start_row, start_col, 0)
    
    # Check if we have infinite cycles
    has_infinite = False
    max_sequences = 0
    
    for d_row, d_col in d_positions:
        sequences = get_max_sequences(d_row, d_col)
        if sequences == float('inf'):
            has_infinite = True
            break
        max_sequences = max(max_sequences, sequences)
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_sequences == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_sequences)
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    # If no D found, can't start
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # For each starting D position, we'll find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
    # We'll use DFS with state (row, col, phase) and detect cycles
    
    def dfs(row, col, phase, visited):
        # phase: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # visited: set of visited states (row, col, phase)
        
        state = (row, col, phase)
        if state in visited:
            return float('inf')  # Found a cycle -> infinite
        
        visited.add(state)
        
        # Find next letter in sequence
        next_phase = phase + 1
        if next_phase == 4:
            # We've completed one DIMA (phase 0->1->2->3->0)
            next_phase = 0
            # Now we need to find a D to continue the sequence
            # Look for adjacent D cells
            next_d_moves = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                    next_d_moves.append((nr, nc))
            
            # If we can find a D, we can potentially continue infinitely
            if next_d_moves:
                # Try each possible next D
                max_from_next = 0
                for nr, nc in next_d_moves:
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 0, visited.copy())
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                    max_from_next = max(max_from_next, result)
                return 1 + max_from_next
            else:
                return 1
        else:
            # Move to next letter
            next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
            next_moves = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    next_moves.append((nr, nc))
            
            if not next_moves:
                # Can't proceed
                return 0
            
            max_from_next = 0
            for nr, nc in next_moves:
                result = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase, visited.copy())
                max_from_next = max(max_from_next, result)
            return max_from_next
    
    # Check for infinite cycles
    has_infinite = False
    max_sequences = 0
    
    for d_row, d_col in d_positions:
        # Start DFS from this D position
        sequences = dfs(d_row, d_col, 0, set())
        if sequences == float('inf'):
            has_infinite = True
            break
        max_sequences = max(max_sequences, sequences)
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_sequences == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_sequences)
```

    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())

    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # For each starting D position, we'll find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
    # We'll use DFS with state (row, col, phase) and detect cycles

        visited.add(state)
        
        # Find next letter in sequence

            next_phase = 0

            # Look for adjacent D cells
            next_d_moves = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                    next_d_moves.append((nr, nc))
            
            # If we can find a D, we can potentially continue infinitely
            if next_d_moves:
                # Try each possible next D
                max_from_next = 0
                for nr, nc in next_d_moves:
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 0, visited.copy())
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                    max_from_next = max(max_from_next, result)
                return 1 + max_from_next
            else:
                return 1
        else:
            # Move to next letter
            next_letter = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_phase]
            next_moves = []
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    next_moves.append((nr, nc))
            
            if not next_moves:
                # Can't proceed
                return 0
            
            max_from_next = 0
            for nr, nc in next_moves:
                result = dfs(nr, nc, next_phase, visited.copy())
                max_from_next = max(max_from_next, result)
            return max_from_next
    
    # Check for infinite cycles
    has_infinite = False
    max_sequences = 0
    
    for d_row, d_col in d_positions:
        # Start DFS from this D position
        sequences = dfs(d_row, d_col, 0, set())
        if sequences == float('inf'):
            has_infinite = True
            break
        max_sequences = max(max_sequences, sequences)
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_sequences == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_sequences)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to traverse the sequence "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. The traversal follows a strict pattern: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. She can only move to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of times she can go through "DIMA"
6. Three possible outputs:
   - "Poor Dima!" if she can't go through "DIMA" even once
   - "Poor Inna!" if she can go through it infinitely
   - A positive integer if she can go through it a finite number of times

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a graph traversal problem where we have states D, I, M, A
2. We can model this as a directed graph where each cell with a letter points to its neighbors with the next letter in sequence
3. We need to find if there's a cycle that allows infinite traversal
4. If there's a cycle (especially involving D→I→M→A→D), we get infinite loops
5. We need to check if any path leads to a cycle

## Approach

1. Use graph traversal (like DFS/BFS) to find all possible paths
2. Use visited states to detect cycles
3. For each starting position with 'D', we can trace the path D→I→M→A→D→...
4. We need to track visited positions to detect cycles
5. If we encounter a state (position, current_letter) that we've seen before, we have a cycle
6. We can use a state (row, col, letter) to track our position and current state in the sequence

## Algorithm Steps

1. For each cell with 'D':
   - Try to traverse the sequence D→I→M→A→D→...
   - Use DFS with memoization to avoid infinite loops
   - Track visited states (row, col, current_letter) to detect cycles
   - If we find a cycle, return "Poor Inna!"
   - Otherwise, calculate the maximum path length

2. If no valid path exists (can't find D→I→M→A), return "Poor Dima!"

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # DP table to store the maximum times we can go through DIMA from each state
    # state: (row, col, current_letter_index)
    # current_letter_index: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
    # We store the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles we can achieve
    
    # Alternatively, we can use DFS with memoization to detect cycles
    # We'll use a visited set to detect cycles
    
    def dfs(row, col, phase, visited_states):
        # phase: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # When phase == 0, we're at D (start), when phase == 3, we're at A
        
        # If we've seen this state before, we have a cycle
        state = (row, col, phase)
        if state in visited_states:
            return float('inf')  # infinite cycle
        
        visited_states.add(state)
        
        # Find the next letter we need
        next_phase = (phase + 1) % 4
        next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_phase]
        
        # If we can't move to the next letter, we're done for this path
        next_pos = None
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                next_pos = (nr, nc)
                break
        
        # If we can move to the next letter, continue
        if next_pos:
            next_row, next_col = next_pos
            result = dfs(next_row, next_col, next_phase, visited_states)
            if result == float('inf'):
                return float('inf')
            # If we have a finite result, we increment the count
            return result + 1 if result != float('inf') else 0
        else:
            # We can't continue, this path ends here
            return 0
    
    # Check if we can go through DIMA at all
    max_times = 0
    found_cycle = False
    
    # Try starting from each D cell
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                visited_states = set()
                result = dfs(i, j, 0, visited_states)
                if result == float('inf'):
                    found_cycle = True
                else:
                    max_times = max(max_times, result)
    
    if found_cycle:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_times == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_times)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.


-----Examples-----
Input
1 2
DI

Output
Poor Dima!

Input
2 2
MA
ID

Output
Poor Inna!

Input
5 5
DIMAD
DIMAI
DIMAM
DDMAA
AAMID

Output
4

In the first test sample, Inna cannot go through name DIMA a single time.

In the second test sample, Inna can go through the infinite number of words DIMA. For that, she should move in the clockwise direction starting from the lower right corner.

In the third test sample the best strategy is to start from the cell in the upper left corner of the table. ## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with letters D, I, M, A

4. Movements are only to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of times she can go through "DIMA"

## Key Insights

1. This is a graph problem where we model each cell as a node and edges exist between cells that form the DIMA sequence
2. We need to find cycles in this graph - specifically, cycles that can be traversed repeatedly
3. If there's a cycle, Inna can go through DIMA infinitely many times → "Poor Inna!"
4. If there's no path at all, output "Poor Dima!"
5. Otherwise, find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences we can traverse

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Memoization: memo[row][col][step] = maximum cycles we can complete from this state
    # step: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
    memo = {}
    
    def dfs(row, col, step):
        # State: (row, col, step)
        state = (row, col, step)
        if state in memo:
            return memo[state]
        
        # If we've completed a full DIMA cycle, we can potentially start another
        # But we need to check if we can continue from A to D
        
        # For current step, what is the next expected letter?
        next_step = (step + 1) % 4
        next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_step]
        
        # If we're at A (step 3), we can go back to D (step 0) to start another cycle
        if step == 3:
            # We just completed one DIMA sequence, so we can count this
            # But we need to see if we can continue from A to D
            result = 1  # We've completed 1 DIMA
            
            # Try to move from A to D in adjacent cells
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == "D":
                    res_from_next = dfs(nr, nc, 0)
                    # The total cycles would be 1 (completed) + cycles from new D
                    if res_from_next > result:
                        result = res_from_next
            memo[state] = result
            return result
        
        # Otherwise, we just need to continue the path
        max_cycles = 0
        
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                cycles_from_here = dfs(nr, nc, next_step)
                # We don't add 1 here because we only count when we complete a full cycle
                # The increment happens when we reach step 3
                if cycles_from_here > max_cycles:
                    max_cycles = cycles_from_here
        
        memo[state] = max_cycles
        return max_cycles
    
    # Check if there's any valid starting position
    found_any_path = False
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == "D":
                found_any_path = True
                # Try DFS from this position
                try:
                    result = dfs(i, j, 0)
                    # If we get a finite result, that's our answer
                    print(result)
                    return
                except:
                    pass
    
    # If no D found
    if not found_any_path:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # We need to detect if there's a cycle
    # Actually, let me reconsider - the DFS above doesn't properly detect cycles
    
    # Alternative approach: we need to detect if we can have infinite paths
    # We'll use a different state: (row, col, step) and track if we've visited it before
    # If we visit the same state again, we have a cycle
    
    def has_cycle():
        visited = {}
        
        def dfs_with_cycle_detection(row, col, step, path):
            state = (row, col, step)
            if state in visited:
                return True  # We've seen this state before - cycle detected
            
            visited[state] = path
            
            # If we're at A (step 3), we can go back to D, so we need to check if we can continue
            if step == 3:
                next_step = 0
                next_letter = "D"
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        if dfs_with_cycle_detection(nr, nc, next_step, path + [(nr, nc, next_step)]):
                            return True
            else:
                next_step = (step + 1) % 4
                next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_step]
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        if dfs_with_cycle_detection(nr, nc, next_step, path + [(nr, nc, next_step)]):
                            return True
            return False
        
        # Try from each D position
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == "D":
                    # Clear visited for each start
                    current_visited = {}
                    if dfs_with_cycle_detection(i, j, 0, []):
                        return True
        return False
    
    # Check for cycles first
    if has_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # If no cycle and no path, print poor Dima
    found_d = False
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == "D":
                found_d = True
                break
        if found_d:
            break
    
    if not found_d:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # Otherwise, find maximum cycles from each D position
    max_cycles = 0
    
    def dfs_count(row, col, step):
        state = (row, col, step)
        if step == 3:
            # We've completed one DIMA sequence
            return 1
        
        max_count = 0
        next_step = (step + 1) % 4
        next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_step]
        
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = row + dr, col + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                count = dfs_count(nr, nc, next_step)
                if count > max_count:
                    max_count = count
        
        return max_count
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == "D":
                cycles = dfs_count(i, j, 0)
                if cycles > max_cycles:
                    max_cycles = cycles
    
    print(max_cycles)
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

    # We need to detect if we can have infinite paths
    # If we can return to a previously visited state in the sequence, we have a cycle
    
    def has_infinite_path():
        # Use visited set to detect cycles
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, step, path):
            # State is (r, c, step)
            state = (r, c, step)
            if state in visited:
                return True  # Found a cycle
            
            visited.add(state)

            if step == 3:

                next_letter = "D"
                for dr, dc in directions:

                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        if dfs(nr, nc, next_step, path + [(nr, nc, next_step)]):
                            return True
            else:
                next_step = (step + 1) % 4
                next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_step]
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        if dfs(nr, nc, next_step, path + [(nr, nc, next_step)]):
                            return True
            return False
        
        # Try from each D position
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == "D":
                    # Need to clear visited for each start
                    visited.clear()
                    if dfs(i, j, 0, []):
                        return True
        return False

    has_d = any(grid[i][j] == "D" for i in range(n) for j in range(m))

        print("Poor Dima!")
        return

    if has_infinite_path():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # No infinite paths, so find maximum number of DIMA sequences
    max_cycles = 0

        # Returns maximum number of complete DIMA sequences from this state
        if step == 3:
            # We completed one DIMA sequence
            # We can potentially start another from the next D
            # But we need to see what happens after A
            next_step = 0
            next_letter = "D"
            max_seq = 1  # We completed one sequence
            
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    res = max_sequences(nr, nc, next_step)
                    if res > max_seq:
                        max_seq = res
            return max_seq
        
        next_step = (step + 1) % 4
        next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_step]

                    max_seq = res
        
        return max_seq

            if grid[i][j] == "D":
```

        grid.append(input().strip())

            return max_seq
        
        next_step = (step + 1) % 4
        next_letter = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_step]
        
        max_seq = 0
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                res = max_sequences(nr, nc, next_step)
                if res > max_seq:
                    max_seq = res
        
        return max_seq
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == "D":
                cycles = max_sequences(i, j, 0)
                if cycles > max_cycles:
                    max_cycles = cycles
    
    print(max_cycles)

solve()

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters 'D', 'I', 'M', 'A'
2. Inna needs to go through the sequence "DIMA" repeatedly
3. The path must follow: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. She can only move to adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of times she can go through "DIMA"
6. Three possible outputs:
   - "Poor Dima!" if she can't go through "DIMA" even once
   - "Poor Inna!" if she can go through it infinitely
   - A positive integer if she can go through it a finite number of times

## Key Insights

1. This is a graph traversal problem where each cell is a node, and edges exist between adjacent cells with consecutive letters in "DIMA"
2. The sequence forms a cycle: D → I → M → A → D
3. If there's a cycle that can be traversed repeatedly, we have infinite possibilities
4. We need to find if there's any cycle in the DIMA graph
5. We can use DFS/BFS to find paths and detect cycles

## Approach

1. Build a directed graph where edges go from D→I, I→M, M→A, A→D
2. Use DFS to find all possible paths starting from each 'D' cell
3. Track visited cells to detect cycles
4. For each path, count how many complete "DIMA" cycles we can make
5. If we find a cycle (can go through DIMA infinitely), return "Poor Inna!"
6. If no path exists, return "Poor Dima!"
7. Otherwise, return the maximum number of complete cycles

## Algorithm

1. Create a graph representation
2. For each 'D' cell, do DFS/BFS to find the longest path that can form DIMA cycles
3. Keep track of visited states (position and current step in DIMA sequence)
4. If we can return to a previous state, we have a cycle → infinite
5. Otherwise, count the maximum number of complete cycles

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all starting positions with 'D'
    start_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                start_positions.append((i, j))
    
    # If no starting position with 'D', can't start
    if not start_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # Check if there's a cycle that allows infinite DIMA sequences
    # We'll use DFS with state (row, col, current_letter_index)
    # current_letter_index: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
    visited = set()
    
    def dfs(r, c, step, path_count):
        # step: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # path_count: current number of complete DIMA cycles (we count when we complete A->D)
        
        # If we've seen this state before, we have a cycle
        state = (r, c, step)
        if state in visited:
            return float('inf')  # indicates infinite cycles
            
        visited.add(state)
        
        # If step is 3 (we just reached A), we need to go to D
        # We can complete one DIMA cycle
        if step == 3:
            # Now we need to find D to continue
            next_step = 0
            next_r, next_c = r, c
            # Try all adjacent cells
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                    # Found a D, we can continue
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, next_step, path_count + 1)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                    # We can return the result from this path
            # If no D found, we can't continue
            return path_count if path_count > 0 else 0
        
        # Move to next required letter
        next_step = step + 1
        if next_step == 4:  # We've completed a cycle
            next_step = 0
        
        # Look for cell with the next required letter
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                if step == 0 and grid[nr][nc] == 'I':  # D -> I
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 1, path_count)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                elif step == 1 and grid[nr][nc] == 'M':  # I -> M
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 2, path_count)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                elif step == 2 and grid[nr][nc] == 'A':  # M -> A
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 3, path_count)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                elif step == 3 and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':  # A -> D
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 0, path_count + 1)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
        
        # If we get here, no valid next move
        return path_count
    
    # Try starting from each D cell
    max_cycles = 0
    infinite = False
    
    for r, c in start_positions:
        # Since we start at D (step 0), we need to go to I
        result = dfs(r, c, 0, 0)
        if result == float('inf'):
            infinite = True
        else:
            max_cycles = max(max_cycles, result)
    
    if infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    elif max_cycles == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)

solve()
```

```python
    has_cycle = False

        if state in visited:
            has_cycle = True
            return float('inf')  # indicates infinite cycles

                        has_cycle = True

            # If no D found, we can't continue
            return current_cycles

        next_step = step + 1
        if next_step == 4:
            next_step = 0

                    result = dfs(nr, nc, 1, current_cycles)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        has_cycle = True
                        return float('inf')

                    if result == float('inf'):
                        has_cycle = True
                        return float('inf')

                    if result == float('inf'):
                        has_cycle = True
                        return float('inf')
        
        # No valid next move
        return current_cycles

        result = dfs(r, c, 0, 0)

            has_cycle = True
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to go through the name "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. The path must follow the sequence: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. Movement is only to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of complete "DIMA" sequences

## Key Insights

1. If Inna can't find a complete "DIMA" sequence once, output "Poor Dima!"
2. If she can loop infinitely (like in a cycle), output "Poor Inna!"
3. Otherwise, output the maximum finite number of times

## Analysis

This is essentially a graph problem where:
- Each cell is a node
- There are directed edges from D→I, I→M, M→A, A→D
- We want the maximum number of complete cycles (DIMA sequences)

The key insight is that if there's a cycle that allows Inna to go from D→I→M→A→D, she can repeat this infinitely.

So we need to:
1. Find all possible paths that follow D→I→M→A→D
2. Check if there exists a cycle (infinite loop)
3. If not, find the maximum finite path

## Approach

1. Use DFS/BFS to find all possible paths from each 'D' cell
2. For each path, count how many complete "DIMA" sequences we can make
3. Check if any path forms a cycle (which would lead to infinite repetitions)

However, since we're looking for maximum number of repetitions, and cycles would make it infinite, we need to detect cycles.

Actually, let me think differently:

- If from any cell we can reach a cycle (where we can go D→I→M→A→D), then we have infinite possibility
- Otherwise, we find the maximum finite path

We can use dynamic programming with state (row, col, current_letter) to track the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences we can achieve.

But more simply:
1. For each starting D cell, we can try to find the maximum number of DIMA sequences we can form
2. We need to detect cycles - if there's a cycle in the path, then infinite
3. Otherwise, return the maximum finite count

Wait, actually we need to detect if there's a loop that can be traversed infinitely. This happens when we can go from a state to another state and eventually return to a previous state in the path.

Better approach:
- Use DFS with memoization where state is (row, col, current_letter)
- Track the path to detect cycles
- If we revisit a state with the same current_letter, we have a cycle - so infinite
- Otherwise, calculate the maximum sequence count

## Algorithm

1. For each 'D' cell, start a DFS/BFS to find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
2. Use memoization to avoid cycles
3. If we detect a cycle (return to same state with same letter), return infinite
4. Otherwise, return the maximum count

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Memoization: (row, col, current_letter) -> max sequences ending here
    # Also need to track if we've seen this state to detect cycles
    memo = {}
    
    def dfs(row, col, current_letter, visited_states):
        # State is (row, col, current_letter)
        state = (row, col, current_letter)
        if state in visited_states:
            # This means we have a cycle - infinite possibility
            return float('inf')
        
        visited_states.add(state)
        
        # If current_letter is D, we just started or just finished a cycle
        # We need to find the next letter in sequence
        next_letter = None
        if current_letter == 'D':
            next_letter = 'I'
        elif current_letter == 'I':
            next_letter = 'M'
        elif current_letter == 'M':
            next_letter = 'A'
        elif current_letter == 'A':
            next_letter = 'D'
        
        max_sequences = 0
        
        # Try all adjacent cells with the required letter
        for dr, dc in directions:
            new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
            if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m and grid[new_row][new_col] == next_letter:
                # Found a valid move
                # Recursively find sequences from this new position
                sequences_from_next = dfs(new_row, new_col, next_letter, visited_states.copy())
                if sequences_from_next == float('inf'):
                    # If this path has a cycle, infinite is possible
                    return float('inf')
                max_sequences = max(max_sequences, 1 + sequences_from_next)
        
        # If we couldn't move to any valid cell, we can't complete another DIMA
        # But we can still count current sequences
        if max_sequences == 0 and current_letter == 'D':
            # If we're at D and can't move, we can't complete any DIMA
            pass
            
        return max_sequences
    
    # If no D found, can't start
    has_d = any('D' in row for row in grid)
    if not has_d:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_count = 0
    # Try each starting D cell
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                # Start DFS from this D cell
                visited_states = set()
                result = dfs(i, j, 'D', visited_states)
                if result == float('inf'):
                    print("Poor Inna!")
                    return
                max_count = max(max_count, result)
    
    print(max_count)

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.


-----Examples-----
Input
1 2
DI

Output
Poor Dima!

Input
2 2
MA
ID

Output
Poor Inna!

Input
5 5
DIMAD
DIMAI
DIMAM
DDMAA
AAMID

Output
4



-----Note-----

Notes to the samples:

In the first test sample, Inna cannot go through name DIMA a single time.

In the second test sample, Inna can go through the infinite number of words DIMA. For that, she should move in the clockwise direction starting from the lower right corner.

In the third test sample the best strategy is to start from the cell in the upper left corner of the table. ## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m table with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to go through the name "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. The path must follow the sequence: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. Movement is only to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of complete "DIMA" cycles possible

## Key Insights

1. This is a graph traversal problem where each cell can be a node
2. We have directed edges: D→I, I→M, M→A, A→D
3. We need to find cycles in the graph and their lengths
4. If there's a cycle of length 4 (DIMA), we can go through it infinitely → "Poor Inna!"
5. If no valid path exists from any D to complete one cycle → "Poor Dima!"
6. Otherwise, we find the maximum number of complete cycles

## Approach

1. Build a graph representation of the table
2. For each cell with 'D', perform BFS/DFS to find paths that complete a full "DIMA" cycle
3. Track the path length (number of cycles completed)
4. If we find a cycle that can be repeated infinitely, return "Poor Inna!"
5. Otherwise, return the maximum cycles possible

However, there's a key insight: if there's any cycle that can be traversed infinitely, it means we can go through DIMA infinitely many times. This happens when we can form a loop D→I→M→A→D.

Actually, looking at the examples:
- Example 2: "Poor Inna!" - infinite cycles
- Example 1: "Poor Dima!" - impossible to go through once
- Example 3: 4 - finite cycles

The key is: if there exists a cycle that goes D→I→M→A→D (returning to a D), then we can go through DIMA infinitely many times.

So algorithm:
1. Check if there's a valid path from any D to I to M to A to D (completing a cycle)
2. If such a cycle exists, return "Poor Inna!"
3. If no such path exists but some paths exist (finite cycles), find the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles
4. If no path exists from any D to complete a single DIMA, return "Poor Dima!"

## Implementation Strategy

I'll use BFS from each D cell, keeping track of the path length and where we are in the cycle (D, I, M, A).

- For each starting D, do BFS and track the number of complete DIMA cycles we can form

But we need to be careful - we might find a path that completes DIMA once, but then can't continue. We need to check for cycles.

1. Use BFS from each D cell

3. Otherwise, we compute the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles

Actually, the infinite case occurs when there's a cycle that goes through D→I→M→A→D. So if we can go from D to I to M to A and then back to D, we have infinite possibilities.

So:
1. For each D, try to find if there's a path that completes DIMA and returns to D
2. If yes for any D, return "Poor Inna!"

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [-1, 1, 0, 0]
    
    # Check if we can go from position (r, c) to next in sequence
    def can_go(r, c, next_char):
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                return True
        return False
    
    # Check if we can complete a full DIMA cycle from a starting D
    # and whether we can return to D after completing DIMA
    def has_infinite_cycle(start_r, start_c):
        # BFS from start position with state: (r, c, current_char_count)
        # current_char_count: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # We want to see if we can complete DIMA and then go back to D
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([(start_r, start_c, 0)])  # (r, c, state)
        visited.add((start_r, start_c, 0))
        
        while queue:
            r, c, state = queue.popleft()
            
            # Try to move to next character in sequence
            next_state = (state + 1) % 4
            next_char = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_state]
            
            # If we're at state 3 (A), we need to go back to D
            if state == 3:  # We've completed one DIMA cycle
                # Check if we can go from A to D
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        # We can go back to D, so we have infinite cycles
                        return True
            
            # Try to move to next character
            if can_go(r, c, next_char):
                nr, nc = r, c
                # Find a valid move to next_char
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                        new_state = (state + 1) % 4
                        if (nr, nc, new_state) not in visited:
                            visited.add((nr, nc, new_state))
                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_state))
        
        return False
    
    # Check if we can complete at least one DIMA cycle from any D
    infinite_found = False
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                if has_infinite_cycle(i, j):
                    infinite_found = True
                    break
        if infinite_found:
            break
    
    if infinite_found:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    else:
        # Find maximum number of complete DIMA cycles from any D
        max_cycles = 0
        
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # BFS to find how many complete DIMA cycles we can make
                    visited = set()
                    queue = deque([(i, j, 0)])  # (r, c, state)
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while queue:
                        r, c, state = queue.popleft()
                        
                        # Try to move to next character
                        next_state = (state + 1) % 4
                        next_char = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_state]
                        
                        # If we're at state 3 (A), we complete one DIMA cycle
                        if state == 3:  # We just completed a cycle
                            # We can't continue from A to D (because we already ran through it)
                            # But we just completed one full cycle
                            max_cycles = max(max_cycles, 1)
                        else:
                            # Look for a next character
                            if can_go(r, c, next_char):
                                for dr, dc in directions:
                                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                                        new_state = (state + 1) % 4
                                        if (nr, nc, new_state) not in visited:
                                            visited.add((nr, nc, new_state))
                                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_state))
        
        # Wait, this is not right. We need to count complete cycles.
        # Let me reconsider.
        
        # Actually, we need to track how many complete cycles we've made.
        # We should go through D->I->M->A, that's one cycle.
        # From A, we need to go to D to start another cycle.
        
        # Let's do a different BFS that counts complete cycles
        max_cycles = 0
        
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # BFS to find the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles
                    visited = set()
                    queue = deque([(i, j, 0, 0)])  # (r, c, state, cycles)
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while queue:
                        r, c, state, cycles = queue.popleft()
                        
                        # Try to move to next character
                        next_state = (state + 1) % 4
                        next_char = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_state]
                        
                        # If we're at state 3 (A), we've completed a cycle
                        if state == 3:
                            # Now check if we can go back to D to start next cycle
                            can_continue = False
                            for dr, dc in directions:

                                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                                    can_continue = True
                                    break
                            
                            if can_continue:
                                # We can complete another cycle
                                new_cycles = cycles + 1
                                # But we don't know if we can continue infinitely
                                # Actually, if we can go from A back to D, we have infinite

                                # So we can only complete cycles until we can't go to D
                                # We just made one additional cycle

                                # Actually, we need to track the entire path

                                    if (nr, nc, new_state) not in visited:
                                        visited.add((nr, nc, new_state))
                                        queue.append((nr, nc, new_state, new_cycles))

        # But if we can go from A to D, then we have infinite cycles.
        # So in our initial check, if any D can reach a path that goes D->I->M->A->D, we have infinite.
        
        # For the finite case, we just need to find how many complete DIMA cycles we can make.
        # A complete cycle is D->I->M->A.
        
        # We don't need to go back to D after A - that would be a new cycle.
        # The problem says: from A she always goes to D. So each "DIMA" requires going from D to I to M to A.
        
        # Actually re-reading: "from the letter 'A' she always goes to the letter 'D'"
        # And "Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter 'D'"
        
        # So the path is: D -> I -> M -> A -> D -> I -> M -> A -> ...
        
        # So a complete "DIMA" is one D->I->M->A.
        
        # Maximum number of times she can go through named DIMA is the number of times she completes this sequence.
        
        # So we need to find, for each starting D, the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences we can form.
        
        # But we can go through a sequence multiple times if we can return to D after A.
        
        # But if we can return to D after A, we have infinite cycles.
        
        # So we already know if there's infinite cycles.
        
        # For the finite case, we just need to find the maximum number of complete D->I->M->A sequences.
        
        # Since we don't have infinite cycles, once we go from A to D (and that D is not part of a cycle), we stop.

        # So if we don't have such cycles, then once we complete a DIMA sequence, we cannot complete another.
        
        # Therefore, the answer is simply the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences (D->I->M->A) we can form from any starting D.
        
        # But can we form a sequence from a D and then from A go to another D? Only if from A there's a path to D, but that would create a cycle.

            for j in range(m):

                        if state == 3:

                            # But from A, we can go to D, and then start a new cycle
                            # However, if we can go from A to D, we have infinite cycles
                            # But we already checked for infinite cycles, so we shouldn't be able to go from A to D
                            # So we just complete one cycle and stop?

                            # But we already checked that there's no infinite cycle
                            # So we can't go from A to D
                            # Therefore, we can only complete one cycle
                            # But wait, maybe we can compute the next steps without going back to D?

                            # From A, we need to go to D to start the next cycle.
                            # But if we can go from A to D, we have infinite cycles.
                            # Since we already checked and there are no infinite cycles, we cannot go from A to D.
                            # So we cannot complete another cycle.

                            # We can only count this cycle.
                            # So the maximum cycles from this path is cycles + 1?
                            # But we're at state 3, which means we've just completed a cycle.
                            # So we should update.

                            # But only if we can reach back to D from A.

                            # So we can update max_cycles.

                            pass

                        if can_go(r, c, next_char):

                                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                                    new_state = (state + 1) % 4
                                    new_cycles = cycles

                                    # and we can count it

                                        visited.add((nr, nc, new_state))

        # Let me start over with a simpler approach.

        # So for each starting D, we want to find the maximum number of complete D->I->M->A sequences we can make.
        
        # We can do BFS from each D and keep track of the current state (0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A).
        # When we reach state 3 (A), we have completed one cycle.
        # From A, we cannot go to D (because that would create an infinite cycle, which we already ruled out).
        # So once we reach A, we stop there.
        
        # So we just need to count how many times we reach state 3.
        
        max_cycles = 0
        
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # BFS from (i, j)
                    visited = set()
                    queue = deque([(i, j, 0)])  # (r, c, state)
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while queue:
                        r, c, state = queue.popleft()

                        next_state = (state + 1) % 4

                        if next_state == 3:

                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while queue:

                            # So since we don't have infinite cycles, we shouldn't be able to go from A to D

                    queue = deque([(i, j, 0, 0)])  # (r, c, state, cycles)
                    visited[(i, j, 0)] = 0
```

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Function to check if we can go from (r, c) to a character
    def can_go(r, c, char):
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == char:
                return True
        return False
    
    # Check if there's an infinite cycle (D->I->M->A->D)
    def has_infinite_cycle():
        # We'll do BFS from each D cell
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # BFS state: (r, c, state) where state is 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
                    visited = set()
                    queue = deque([(i, j, 0)])
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while queue:
                        r, c, state = queue.popleft()
                        
                        # Try to go to next state
                        next_state = (state + 1) % 4
                        next_char = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_state]
                        
                        # If next_state is 3 (A), we've completed one cycle
                        # After A, we need to go to D to start another cycle
                        # So if we can go from current position to a D, we have an infinite cycle
                        if next_state == 3:  # At A
                            # Check if we can go from (r, c) to any D
                            for dr, dc in directions:
                                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                                    # We can go back to D, so we have infinite cycle
                                    return True
                            
                            # But we need to continue to see if we can complete more cycles
                            # Actually, if we can go from A to D, we have infinite cycle
                            # So we return True immediately
                            
                        # Try to move to next_char
                        if can_go(r, c, next_char):
                            for dr, dc in directions:
                                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                                    new_state = (state + 1) % 4
                                    if (nr, nc, new_state) not in visited:
                                        visited.add((nr, nc, new_state))
                                        queue.append((nr, nc, new_state))
        return False
    
    # Check for infinite cycles
    if has_infinite_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
    else:
        # Find maximum number of complete DIMA cycles
        max_cycles = 0
        
        # For each D, do BFS to count how many complete DIMA cycles we can make
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # BFS with state (r, c, state) and cycle count
                    # We'll use a visited set to avoid cycles
                    visited = set()
                    queue = deque([(i, j, 0, 0)])  # (r, c, state, cycles)
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while queue:
                        r, c, state, cycles = queue.popleft()
                        
                        # Try to go to next state
                        next_state = (state + 1) % 4
                        next_char = ["D", "I", "M", "A"][next_state]
                        
                        # If we're at state 3 (A), we've completed one cycle
                        if next_state == 3:
                            # We complete one cycle
                            new_cycles = cycles + 1
                            # But we cannot go from A to D (no infinite cycle), so we stop
                            max_cycles = max(max_cycles, new_cycles)
                        else:
                            # Try to move to next_char
                            if can_go(r, c, next_char):
                                for dr, dc in directions:
                                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                                        new_state = (state + 1) % 4
                                        new_cycles = cycles
                                        
                                        if (nr, nc, new_state) not in visited:
                                            visited.add((nr, nc, new_state))
                                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_state, new_cycles))
        
        if max_cycles == 0:
            print("Poor Dima!")
        else:
            print(max_cycles)
```

Wait, there's a bug in the infinite cycle detection. When we're at state 3 (A), we check if we can go to D, but we're already at a cell. The move from A to D should be from the current (r,c) to a D cell.

Also, in the BFS, when we are at a cell with state 3, we should check if we can go to a D, and if so, return True.

```python
from collections import deque

grid = []
for _ in range(n):
    grid.append(input().strip())
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Let \mathrm{popcount}(n) be the number of `1`s in the binary representation of n. For example, \mathrm{popcount}(3) = 2, \mathrm{popcount}(7) = 3, and \mathrm{popcount}(0) = 0.

Let f(n) be the number of times the following operation will be done when we repeat it until n becomes 0: "replace n with the remainder when n is divided by \mathrm{popcount}(n)." (It can be proved that, under the constraints of this problem, n always becomes 0 after a finite number of operations.)

For example, when n=7, it becomes 0 after two operations, as follows:

* \mathrm{popcount}(7)=3, so we divide 7 by 3 and replace it with the remainder, 1.
* \mathrm{popcount}(1)=1, so we divide 1 by 1 and replace it with the remainder, 0.



You are given an integer X with N digits in binary. For each integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq N, let X_i be what X becomes when the i-th bit from the top is inverted. Find f(X_1), f(X_2), \ldots, f(X_N).

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
* X is an integer with N digits in binary, possibly with leading zeros.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N
X


Output

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a function `popcount(n)` that counts the number of 1s in binary representation of n
2. We have a function `f(n)` that counts how many times we need to apply the operation: replace n with n % popcount(n) until n becomes 0
3. We're given a binary string X of N digits
4. For each position i (1 to N), we create X_i by flipping the i-th bit from the top, then compute f(X_i)

## Key Observations

1. When we flip a single bit in a binary number, we only change the value by either adding or subtracting a power of 2 (the value at that position)
2. The `f(n)` function has some interesting properties:
   - If a number has popcount = 1, then n % 1 = 0, so f(n) = 1
   - If a number has popcount = 2, then we do n % 2, which gives us 0 if n is even, or 1 if n is odd
   - After one operation, if we get 1, then f(n) = 2 (since f(1) = 1)
   - The function `f(n)` is relatively small for most values

3. The key insight: when we flip a bit, we change the popcount by ±1 (if we were at a 1→0 or 0→1 position). 

4. If we flip a bit in a position that was previously 1, we reduce popcount by 1. If we flip a bit that was 0, we increase popcount by 1.

5. After flipping, we need to compute f(X_i) for each case.

## Algorithm

1. Parse input to get N and binary string X
2. For each position i from 0 to N-1:
   - Create X_i by flipping the i-th bit
   - Convert X_i to decimal value
   - Compute f(X_i) by simulating the process until n becomes 0
3. Output the results

## Implementation Details

The function to compute f(n):
- While n > 0:
  - Calculate popcount of n
  - Set n = n % popcount(n)
  - Increment counter
- Return counter

```python
def popcount(n):
    return bin(n).count('1')

def f(n):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    count = 0
    while n > 0:
        pc = popcount(n)
        n = n % pc
        count += 1
    return count

# Read input
N = int(input())
X = input().strip()

# For each position i, flip the i-th bit and compute f(X_i)
for i in range(N):
    # Create new binary string by flipping i-th bit (0-indexed from left)
    if X[i] == '0':
        new_bit = '1'
    else:
        new_bit = '0'
    
    # Build new binary string
    new_X = X[:i] + new_bit + X[i+1:]
    
    # Convert to integer
    n = int(new_X, 2)
    
    # Compute f(n)
    result = f(n)
    print(result)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Let \mathrm{popcount}(n) be the number of `1`s in the binary representation of n. For example, \mathrm{popcount}(3) = 2, \mathrm{popcount}(7) = 3, and \mathrm{popcount}(0) = 0.

Let f(n) be the number of times the following operation will be done when we repeat it until n becomes 0: "replace n with the remainder when n is divided by \mathrm{popcount}(n)." (It can be proved that, under the constraints of this problem, n always becomes 0 after a finite number of operations.)

For example, when n=7, it becomes 0 after two operations, as follows:

* \mathrm{popcount}(7)=3, so we divide 7 by 3 and replace it with the remainder, 1.
* \mathrm{popcount}(1)=1, so we divide 1 by 1 and replace it with the remainder, 0.



You are given an integer X with N digits in binary. For each integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq N, let X_i be what X becomes when the i-th bit from the top is inverted. Find f(X_1), f(X_2), \ldots, f(X_N).

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
* X is an integer with N digits in binary, possibly with leading zeros.

2. Convert this binary string to an integer
3. Simulate the f(n) function until n becomes 0, counting steps

The f(n) function simulation:
- While n > 0:
  - count += 1
  - n = n % popcount(n)

## Implementation Considerations

- We need to handle strings with leading zeros
- The simulation might be efficient enough since popcount(n) is typically small

```python
def popcount(n):
    return bin(n).count('1')

def f(n):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    count = 0
    while n > 0:
        count += 1
        pop = popcount(n)
        n = n % pop
    return count

# Read input
N = int(input())
X = input().strip()

# For each position i (1-indexed from top)
for i in range(N):
    # Create new binary string by inverting the i-th bit (0-indexed)
    new_bits = list(X)
    new_bits[i] = '1' if new_bits[i] == '0' else '0'
    new_X = ''.join(new_bits)
    
    # Convert to integer
    n = int(new_X, 2)
    
    # Compute f(n)
    result = f(n)
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a function `popcount(n)` that counts the number of 1s in binary representation of n
2. We have a function `f(n)` that counts how many times we need to replace n with `n % popcount(n)` until n becomes 0
3. We're given a binary string X of N digits
4. For each position i, we create X_i by inverting the i-th bit (from top), and we need to find f(X_i) for each i

## Key Insights

1. When we invert a bit, we're changing one bit from 0 to 1 or 1 to 0
2. The value of `popcount(n)` changes by either +1 (if we turn 0→1) or -1 (if we turn 1→0)
3. The function f(n) depends on the path of reduction: n → n % popcount(n) → ... → 0

## Important Observation

Let me analyze what happens in the reduction process:
- If n = 0, f(0) = 0 (base case)
- If n > 0, we compute r = n % popcount(n), then f(n) = 1 + f(r)

The key insight is that for most numbers, the reduction will quickly converge to small values.

## Pattern Analysis

Let me think about when f(n) is small:
- If n is a power of 2, say n = 2^k, then popcount(n) = 1, so n % 1 = 0, so f(n) = 1
- If n = 1, popcount(1) = 1, so 1 % 1 = 0, so f(1) = 1
- If n = 2, popcount(2) = 1, so 2 % 1 = 0, so f(2) = 1
- If n = 3, popcount(3) = 2, so 3 % 2 = 1, then f(3) = 1 + f(1) = 2
- If n = 4, popcount(4) = 1, so 4 % 1 = 0, so f(4) = 1
- If n = 5, popcount(5) = 2, so 5 % 2 = 1, f(5) = 1 + f(1) = 2
- If n = 6, popcount(6) = 2, so 6 % 2 = 0, f(6) = 1
- If n = 7, popcount(7) = 3, so 7 % 3 = 1, f(7) = 1 + f(1) = 2

I notice a pattern: if the popcount is 1, then f(n) = 1. If the popcount is greater than 1 and n % popcount(n) = 0, then f(n) = 1. Otherwise, f(n) = 1 + f(n % popcount(n)).

Actually, let me check when n % popcount(n) = 0:
- This happens when popcount(n) divides n
- For powers of 2, popcount = 1, and 1 divides any number, so f(power of 2) = 1
- For other numbers, it's rare

But looking at the examples:
- X = "011" → X1 = "111" (f(7) = 2), X2 = "001" (f(1) = 1), X3 = "010" (f(2) = 1)

The pattern seems to be that f(n) is very small (usually 1, 2, or 3).

## Key Insight

I notice that the reduction always leads to a small number quickly. In fact, it seems that f(n) is at most 3 for the values we encounter.

Let me verify with small values:
- f(0) = 0
- f(1) = 1 (1 % 1 = 0)
- f(2) = 1 (2 % 1 = 0) 
- f(3) = 2 (3 % 2 = 1, then f(1) = 1, so total 2)
- f(4) = 1 (4 % 1 = 0)
- f(5) = 2 (5 % 2 = 1, f(1) = 1, so total 2)
- f(6) = 1 (6 % 2 = 0)
- f(7) = 2 (7 % 3 = 1, f(1) = 1, so total 2)
- f(8) = 1 (8 % 1 = 0)

So it seems that:
- If n is a power of 2 or even with popcount 2 and divisible by 2, f(n) = 1
- Otherwise, f(n) = 2 if n % popcount(n) ≥ 1
- The only case where f(n) > 2 seems to be when it leads to a chain
