Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Each day in Berland consists of $n$ hours. Polycarp likes time management. That's why he has a fixed schedule for each day — it is a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ (each $a_i$ is either $0$ or $1$), where $a_i=0$ if Polycarp works during the $i$-th hour of the day and $a_i=1$ if Polycarp rests during the $i$-th hour of the day.

Days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.

What is the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests? It is guaranteed that there is at least one working hour in a day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains $n$ ($1 \le n \le 2\cdot10^5$) — number of hours per day.

The second line contains $n$ integer numbers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ ($0 \le a_i \le 1$), where $a_i=0$ if the $i$-th hour in a day is working and $a_i=1$ if the $i$-th hour is resting. It is guaranteed that $a_i=0$ for at least one $i$.


-----Output-----

Print the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests. Remember that you should consider that days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 0 1 0 1

Output
2

Input
6
0 1 0 1 1 0

Output
2

Input
7
1 0 1 1 1 0 1

Output
3

Input
3
0 0 0

Output
0



-----Note-----

In the first example, the maximal rest starts in last hour and goes to the first hour of the next day.

In the second example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $4$-th to the $5$-th hour.

In the third example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $3$-rd to the $5$-th hour.

In the fourth example, Polycarp has no rest at all.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 means working and 1 means resting
2. Days repeat endlessly
3. We need to find the maximum number of continuous resting hours (where a_i = 1)
4. Important: Because days repeat, we might have continuous resting hours that span across day boundaries

## Key Insights

1. Within a single day, we can find contiguous segments of 1s
2. But we also need to consider segments that cross from the end of one day to the beginning of the next
3. For example, if the last few hours of a day are resting, and the first few hours of the next day are also resting, we might get a longer continuous sequence

## Approach

1. First, find all contiguous segments of 1s within a single day
2. Check if there are any segments that span across day boundaries
3. For crossing segments: if the last k hours of the day are resting (all 1s), and the first m hours of the day are resting (all 1s), then we can have a continuous sequence of length k + m

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input
2. Find the longest contiguous sequence of 1s within one day
3. Check if there's a sequence that spans across days:
   - Count consecutive 1s from the end of the array
   - Count consecutive 1s from the beginning of the array
   - The total crossing sequence would be (end_consecutive_1s) + (start_consecutive_1s)
4. Return the maximum of these two

## Edge Cases

- If there are no resting hours, return 0
- The examples show that crossing day boundaries can give longer sequences

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]
- Single day segments: [1] (length 1), [1] (length 1)
- End of day: last element is 1, so 1 consecutive 1 from end
- Start of day: first element is 1, so 1 consecutive 1 from start
- Crossing sequence: 1 + 1 = 2
- Maximum: max(1, 1, 2) = 2 ✓

**Example 2**: [0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]
- Single day segments: [1] (length 1), [1, 1] (length 2)
- End of day: last element is 0, so 0 consecutive 1s from end
- Start of day: first element is 0, so 0 consecutive 1s from start
- Crossing sequence: 0 + 0 = 0
- Maximum: max(1, 2, 0) = 2 ✓

**Example 3**: [1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]
- Single day segments: [1] (length 1), [1,1,1] (length 3)
- End of day: last element is 1, but second last is 0, so only 1 consecutive 1 from end
- Start of day: first element is 1, so 1 consecutive 1 from start
- Crossing sequence: 1 + 1 = 2
- Maximum: max(1, 3, 2) = 3 ✓

**Example 4**: [0, 0, 0]
- All are 0, so no resting hours
- Maximum: 0 ✓

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find the maximum length of consecutive 1s within a single day
max_in_day = 0
current = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current += 1
        max_in_day = max(max_in_day, current)
    else:
        current = 0

# Find consecutive 1s from the end of the day
end_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        end_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# Find consecutive 1s from the beginning of the day
start_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        start_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# The maximum possible continuous resting hours (across days)
# is the sum of consecutive 1s from the end and start
crossing_consecutive = end_consecutive + start_consecutive

# Answer is the maximum of within-day segments and across-day segments
result = max(max_in_day, crossing_consecutive)

print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. In the crossing case, if the end has k consecutive 1s and the start has m consecutive 1s, then those can form a continuous sequence of length k+m when we go from end of one day to start of next.

But is this always valid? Yes, because the days repeat. So if the last k hours are resting and the first m hours are resting, then in the sequence ... (last k hours) + (first m hours) of the next day, we have k+m continuous resting hours.

The answer should be the maximum of:
1. The longest contiguous sequence of 1s within a single day
2. The sequence that crosses day boundaries (end_consecutive + start_consecutive)

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find the maximum length of consecutive 1s within a single day
max_in_day = 0
current = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current += 1
        max_in_day = max(max_in_day, current)
    else:
        current = 0

# Find consecutive 1s from the end of the day
end_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        end_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# Find consecutive 1s from the beginning of the day
start_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        start_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# The maximum continuous resting hours (could span days)
crossing_consecutive = end_consecutive + start_consecutive

# Answer is the maximum of within-day and across-day sequences
result = max(max_in_day, crossing_consecutive)

print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Each day in Berland consists of $n$ hours. Polycarp likes time management. That's why he has a fixed schedule for each day — it is a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ (each $a_i$ is either $0$ or $1$), where $a_i=0$ if Polycarp works during the $i$-th hour of the day and $a_i=1$ if Polycarp rests during the $i$-th hour of the day.

Days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.

What is the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests? It is guaranteed that there is at least one working hour in a day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains $n$ ($1 \le n \le 2\cdot10^5$) — number of hours per day.

The second line contains $n$ integer numbers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ ($0 \le a_i \le 1$), where $a_i=0$ if the $i$-th hour in a day is working and $a_i=1$ if the $i$-th hour is resting. It is guaranteed that $a_i=0$ for at least one $i$.


-----Output-----

Print the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests. Remember that you should consider that days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 0 1 0 1

Output
2

Input
6
0 1 0 1 1 0

Output
2

Input
7
1 0 1 1 1 0 1

Output
3

Input
3
0 0 0

Output
0



-----Note-----

In the first example, the maximal rest starts in last hour and goes to the first hour of the next day.

In the second example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $4$-th to the $5$-th hour.

In the third example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $3$-rd to the $5$-th hour.

In the fourth example, Polycarp has no rest at all.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 means working and 1 means resting
2. Days repeat endlessly, so the schedule goes: a₁, a₂, ..., aₙ, a₁, a₂, ...
3. We need to find the maximum number of consecutive resting hours (1s) considering that days wrap around
4. It's guaranteed that there's at least one working hour (so not all are 1s)

## Key Insight

Since days repeat, we need to consider both:
1. Continuous resting hours within a single day
2. Continuous resting hours that cross from the end of one day to the beginning of the next

For example, in the first test case `[1, 0, 1, 0, 1]`:
- Within a day: we have rest periods of length 1 (at positions 2 and 4)
- But we can have rest from hour 5 (which is 1) to hour 1 (which is 1) - that's 2 consecutive rests

## Approach

1. First, find all maximal consecutive rest periods within a single day
2. Check if there's a longer rest period that spans from the end of one day to the beginning of the next

To check for cross-day rest periods:
- We need to count how many consecutive 1s we get when we concatenate the array with itself
- Then find the longest consecutive 1s in this doubled array

However, since we only care about the maximum sequence, we can:
1. Find the maximum length of consecutive 1s in the original array
2. Find if there's a sequence that spans across days by checking the end and beginning

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Calculate the maximum consecutive 1s within the day
2. Check if the last few 1s and first few 1s form a longer sequence when combined

For example, if the array ends with several 1s and starts with several 1s, then those can form a continuous sequence across days.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find maximum consecutive rests within a single day
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

# Check if there's a longer rest period that crosses day boundaries
# We need to check if the ending rests and beginning rests can form a longer sequence
# This happens when the array ends with some 1s and starts with some 1s

# Count consecutive 1s from the end
end_rests = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        end_rests += 1
    else:
        break

# Count consecutive 1s from the beginning
begin_rests = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        begin_rests += 1
    else:
        break

# If both ends have rests, the cross-day sequence length is end_rests + begin_rests
# But we need to make sure it's valid - if it works, the total would be end_rests + begin_rests
# However, since the day has at least one working hour, we don't have to worry about edge cases

# The maximum possible cross-day rest is end_rests + begin_rests
cross_day_rests = end_rests + begin_rests

# But we need to ensure this is only valid when both ends have rests
# Actually, the cross-day sequence might not be longer than the maximum within-day sequence
# We need to return the maximum of all these

result = max_consecutive
if end_rests > 0 and begin_rests > 0:
    # The cross-day sequence is valid and might be longer
    result = max(result, cross_day_rests)

print(result)
```

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: `[1, 0, 1, 0, 1]`
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 1 (at positions 1, 1, 3)
- End rests: from end, we have a[4]=1, so end_rests=1
- Begin rests: a[0]=1, so begin_rests=1
- Cross-day rest = 1+1 = 2
- Result = max(1, 2) = 2 ✓

Example 2: `[0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]`
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 2 (positions 3,4)
- End rests: a[5]=0, so end_rests=0
- Begin rests: a[0]=0, so begin_rests=0
- Cross-day rest = 0+0 = 0
- Result = max(2, 0) = 2 ✓

Example 3: `[1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]`
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 3 (positions 2,3,4)
- End rests: a[6]=1, so end_rests=1
- Begin rests: a[0]=1, so begin_rests=1
- Cross-day rest = 1+1 = 2
- Result = max(3, 2) = 3 ✓

Example 4: `[0, 0, 0]`
- This is a problem - it says "at least one working hour", so this shouldn't happen
- But according to input guarantee: "It is guaranteed that a_i=0 for at least one i"
- In this case, all are 0, so max consecutive rests = 0

My solution works!

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find maximum consecutive rests within a single day
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

# Count consecutive 1s from the end of the day
end_rests = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        end_rests += 1
    else:
        break

# Count consecutive 1s from the beginning of the day
begin_rests = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        begin_rests += 1
    else:
        break

# The maximum rest period could be the cross-day period (if both ends have rests)
cross_day_rests = end_rests + begin_rests

# The answer is the maximum of within-day and cross-day rests
result = max(max_consecutive, cross_day_rests)

print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 means working and 1 means resting
2. Days repeat endlessly
3. We need to find the maximum number of continuous resting hours across all days
4. The key insight is that resting hours can span across day boundaries (from the end of one day to the beginning of the next)

## Key Observations

1. Within a single day, we can find consecutive 1s using a simple scan
2. Across days, we need to consider if there's a sequence that starts at the end of a day and continues at the beginning of the next day
3. For example, if the last few hours of a day are resting (1s) and the first few hours of the next day are also resting (1s), we might get a longer sequence

## Solution Approach

1. First, find the maximum consecutive 1s within a single day
2. Then, check if we can get longer sequences by combining the ending of one day with the beginning of the next
3. To do this, we need to:
   - Count how many consecutive 1s are at the end of the day
   - Count how many consecutive 1s are at the beginning of the day
   - The combined sequence would be (end_resting_count + beginning_resting_count)

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Calculate the maximum consecutive 1s within the array (single day)
2. Calculate consecutive 1s from the end of the array (how many 1s at the end)
3. Calculate consecutive 1s from the beginning of the array (how many 1s at the beginning)
4. The answer is the maximum of these two values (the single-day maximum and the cross-day maximum)

Wait, let me reconsider. The cross-day sequence would be: (consecutive 1s from end of day) + (consecutive 1s from beginning of day). But we need to check if there's a gap.

Actually, let me think more carefully:
- We need to find the longest sequence of consecutive 1s
- This could be entirely within one day, or it could wrap around from the end of one day to the beginning of the next

So the approach:
1. Find the maximum consecutive 1s within a single day
2. Find the consecutive 1s from the end of the day (starting from the end and going backwards)
3. Find the consecutive 1s from the beginning of the day (starting from the beginning and going forwards)
4. The wrap-around sequence would be (end_length + beginning_length)
5. Answer = max(single_day_max, end_length + beginning_length)

But wait - for the wrap-around to work, the day must have at least one 1 at the end and at least one 1 at the beginning.

Actually, let me validate with examples:

Example 1: [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]
- Single day: max consecutive 1s = 1
- End of day (from right): ends with 1, so count from right: 1
- Beginning of day: starts with 1, so count from left: 1
- Wrap-around: 1 + 1 = 2
- Answer: max(1, 2) = 2 ✓

Example 2: [0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]
- Single day: max consecutive 1s = 2 (positions 3,4)
- End of day: ends with 0, so end_length = 0
- Beginning of day: starts with 0, so beginning_length = 0
- Wrap-around: 0 + 0 = 0
- Answer: max(2, 0) = 2 ✓

Example 3: [1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]
- Single day: max consecutive 1s = 3 (positions 3,4,5)
- End of day: ends with 1, so end_length = 1 (last element)
- Beginning of day: starts with 1, so beginning_length = 1
- Wrap-around: 1 + 1 = 2
- Answer: max(3, 2) = 3 ✓

Example 4: [0, 0, 0]
- Single day: max consecutive 1s = 0
- End of day: all 0s, so end_length = 0
- Beginning of day: all 0s, so beginning_length = 0
- Wrap-around: 0 + 0 = 0
- Answer: 0 ✓

The solution seems correct!

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find maximum consecutive 1s within a single day
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

# Find consecutive 1s from the end of the day
end_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        end_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# Find consecutive 1s from the beginning of the day
begin_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        begin_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# The maximum rest time is either within a single day or wrapping around
# For wrapping around, it's end_consecutive + begin_consecutive
max_rest = max(max_consecutive, end_consecutive + begin_consecutive)

print(max_rest)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 means working and 1 means resting
2. Days repeat endlessly
3. We need to find the maximum number of continuous resting hours (consecutive 1s)
4. The key insight is that resting hours can wrap around from the end of one day to the beginning of the next day

## Key Observations

1. First, we need to find the maximum consecutive 1s within a single day
2. We also need to check if there's a longer sequence that wraps around from the end of one day to the beginning of the next
3. For the wrap-around case, we need to check if there's a sequence of 1s that starts at the end of the day and continues at the beginning

## Solution Approach

1. Find the maximum consecutive 1s in the array (without wraparound)
2. Check for wraparound cases:
   - If the last few elements are 1s and the first few elements are 1s, we might have a longer sequence that spans both days
   - Specifically, we need to check if there's a sequence of k consecutive 1s from position i to the end, and from position 0 to j, such that (end - i) + (j - 0) + 1 > current max

3. To handle the wraparound efficiently, we can:
   - Count consecutive 1s from the end
   - Count consecutive 1s from the beginning
   - The wraparound sequence would be (consecutive 1s from end) + (consecutive 1s from beginning) - 1 (since the connection point is counted twice)

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Calculate max_consecutive from a single day (without wraparound)
2. Calculate consecutive 1s from the end (starting from the last element)
3. Calculate consecutive 1s from the beginning (starting from the first element)
4. If both ends have consecutive 1s, the wraparound sequence would be (end_consecutive + begin_consecutive - 1)
5. Return the maximum of these two values

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]
- Single day max: max consecutive 1s = 1
- End consecutive 1s: 1 (last element)
- Begin consecutive 1s: 1 (first element)
- Wraparound: 1 + 1 - 1 = 1
- But the answer is 2, which comes from resting hours from the last hour to the first hour of next day

Wait, let me reconsider. In example 1: [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]
- The remaining hours: 5th hour (1) -> next day 1st hour (1), so we have 2 consecutive resting hours

So we need to check: can we get a sequence that spans from end to beginning?

The wraparound sequence length = (consecutive 1s from end) + (consecutive 1s from beginning) - 1

For [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]:
- consecutive 1s from end: 1 (only last element)
- consecutive 1s from beginning: 1 (only first element)
- wraparound: 1 + 1 - 1 = 1

But answer is 2. Let me check if there's a different interpretation.

Actually, looking at the pattern: in position 5 (last) we have 1, then next day position 1 we have 1. So this forms a connection.

The sequence would be: position 5 (1) + position 1 (1) = 2 hours.

But the consecutive 1s from end: after the 0 at position 4, we have 1 at position 5, so consecutive = 1
From beginning: position 1 has 1, so consecutive = 1

The total wraparound would be (consecutive from end) + (consecutive from beginning) - 1? 

Wait, if we have a sequence ending at position i and a sequence starting at position j where j > i, then we get a wraparound of (end_consecutive + begin_consecutive) if they connect at the boundary.

Actually, let me think differently:

If the last k elements are 1s and the first m elements are 1s, then the total wraparound sequence is (k + m) hours.

In example 1: last 1 element is 1, first 1 element is 1, so 1 + 1 = 2.

In example 2: [0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]
- Max consecutive in a day: 2 (positions 3,4)
- Last element is 0, so no trailing 1s
- First element is 0, so no leading 1s
- Wraparound: 0
- Answer: 2

In example 3: [1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]
- Max consecutive in day: 3 (positions 2,3,4)
- Last element is 1, first 1 is 1
- Trailing 1s: after last 0, so ending at position 5? Let's check: positions 5 is 0, so trailing 1s: from end backwards until 0
- Position 6: 1, position 5: 0, so trailing 1s = 1
- Leading 1s: position 0: 1, position 1: 0, so leading 1s = 1
- Wraparound: 1 + 1 = 2
- Max of (3, 2) = 3 ✓

So the algorithm is:
1. Find max_consecutive in array (without wraparound)
2. Find consecutive 1s from end of array
3. Find consecutive 1s from beginning of array
4. Wraparound_length = trailing_ones + leading_ones
5. Answer = max(max_consecutive, wraparound_length)

Wait, in example 1: trailing_ones = 1 (last element), leading_ones = 1 (first element), so wraparound = 2, which is correct.

In example 1: the array is [1, 0, 1, 0, 1], so first element is 1, last element is 1.

Let me implement:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find max consecutive 1s in a single day
max_consecutive = 0
current = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current)
    else:
        current = 0

# Find consecutive 1s from the end
trailing_ones = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        trailing_ones += 1
    else:
        break

# Find consecutive 1s from the beginning
leading_ones = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        leading_ones += 1
    else:
        break

# The wraparound sequence would be trailing_ones + leading_ones
# Because trailing ones at end can connect to leading ones at beginning
wraparound = trailing_ones + leading_ones

# Answer is the maximum of non-wraparound and wraparound
print(max(max_consecutive, wraparound))
```

Wait, let me verify with example 1:
- a = [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]
- max_consecutive: 
  - i=0: a[0]=1, current=1, max=1
  - i=1: a[1]=0, current=0
  - i=2: a[2]=1, current=1, max=1
  - i=3: a[3]=0, current=0
  - i=4: a[4]=1, current=1, max=1
  - max_consecutive = 1
- trailing_ones: 
  - i=4: a[4]=1, trailing_ones=1
  - i=3: a[3]=0, break
  - trailing_ones = 1
- leading_ones:
  - i=0: a[0]=1, leading_ones=1
  - i=1: a[1]=0, break
  - leading_ones = 1
- wraparound = 1 + 1 = 2
- max(1, 2) = 2 ✓

Example 2: [0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]
- max_consecutive: 
  - i=0: 0, current=0
  - i=1: 1, current=1, max=1
  - i=2: 0, current=0
  - i=3: 1, current=1, max=1
  - i=4: 1, current=2, max=2
  - i=5: 0, current=0
  - max_consecutive = 2
- trailing_ones: 
  - i=5: 0, so trailing_ones = 0
- leading_ones: 
  - i=0: 0, so leading_ones = 0
- wraparound = 0 + 0 = 0
- max(2, 0) = 2 ✓

Example 3: [1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]
- max_consecutive: 
  - i=0: 1, current=1, max=1
  - i=1: 0, current=0
  - i=2: 1, current=1, max=1
  - i=3: 1, current=2, max=2
  - i=4: 1, current=3, max=3
  - i=5: 0, current=0
  - i=6: 1, current=1, max=3
  - max_consecutive = 3
- trailing_ones: 
  - i=6: 1, trailing_ones=1
  - i=5: 0, break
  - trailing_ones = 1
- leading_ones: 
  - i=0: 1, leading_ones=1
  - i=1: 0, break
  - leading_ones = 1
- wraparound = 1 + 1 = 2
- max(3, 2) = 3 ✓

Example 4: [0, 0, 0]
- max_consecutive = 0
- trailing_ones = 0
- leading_ones = 0
- wraparound = 0
- max(0, 0) = 0 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Each day in Berland consists of $n$ hours. Polycarp likes time management. That's why he has a fixed schedule for each day — it is a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ (each $a_i$ is either $0$ or $1$), where $a_i=0$ if Polycarp works during the $i$-th hour of the day and $a_i=1$ if Polycarp rests during the $i$-th hour of the day.

Days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.

What is the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests? It is guaranteed that there is at least one working hour in a day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains $n$ ($1 \le n \le 2\cdot10^5$) — number of hours per day.

The second line contains $n$ integer numbers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ ($0 \le a_i \le 1$), where $a_i=0$ if the $i$-th hour in a day is working and $a_i=1$ if the $i$-th hour is resting. It is guaranteed that $a_i=0$ for at least one $i$.


-----Output-----

Print the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests. Remember that you should consider that days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 0 1 0 1

Output
2

Input
6
0 1 0 1 1 0

Output
2

Input
7
1 0 1 1 1 0 1

Output
3

Input
3
0 0 0

Output
0



-----Note-----

In the first example, the maximal rest starts in last hour and goes to the first hour of the next day.

In the second example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $4$-th to the $5$-th hour.

In the third example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $3$-rd to the $5$-th hour.

In the fourth example, Polycarp has no rest at all.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 = working, 1 = resting
2. Days repeat endlessly
3. We need to find the maximum number of continuous resting hours (1s) considering the cycle
4. The key insight is that continuous rest might span across day boundaries

## Key Observations

1. We need to consider both:
   - Rest periods within a single day
   - Rest periods that cross from the end of one day to the beginning of the next

2. Since days repeat, if we have a rest period at the end of a day and the beginning of the next day, we can combine them.

3. For example, if the day ends with a rest period and the next day starts with a rest period, we might get a longer continuous rest.

## Solution Approach

1. First, find the maximum length of consecutive 1s within a single day.
2. Then, check if we can get longer rests by combining the end of one day with the beginning of the next day.

To check cross-day combinations:
- Find the longest rest period that starts at the end of a day and goes to the beginning of the next day.
- This would be: count of consecutive 1s from the end of the array + count of consecutive 1s from the beginning of the array.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read input
2. Find maximum consecutive 1s within the day
3. Find the length of consecutive 1s from the start (prefix) and from the end (suffix) of the array
4. The cross-day rest length would be (prefix_length + suffix_length)
5. Return the maximum of these two values

Let me trace through examples:

**Example 1: [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]**
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 1
- Prefix (start): 1 (first element)
- Suffix (end): 1 (last element)
- Cross-day: 1 + 1 = 2
- Answer: max(1, 2) = 2 ✓

**Example 2: [0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]**
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 2 (positions 3,4)
- Prefix: 0 (first element is 0)
- Suffix: 0 (last element is 0)
- Cross-day: 0 + 0 = 0
- Answer: max(2, 0) = 2 ✓

**Example 3: [1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]**
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 3 (positions 3,4,5)
- Prefix: 1 (first element)
- Suffix: 1 (last element)
- Cross-day: 1 + 1 = 2
- Answer: max(3, 2) = 3 ✓

**Example 4: [0, 0, 0]**
- Within day: max consecutive 1s = 0
- Prefix: 0
- Suffix: 0
- Cross-day: 0
- Answer: 0 ✓

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find maximum consecutive 1s within a single day
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

# Find consecutive 1s from the start (prefix)
prefix_length = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        prefix_length += 1
    else:
        break

# Find consecutive 1s from the end (suffix)
suffix_length = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        suffix_length += 1
    else:
        break

# Cross-day rest length (from end of one day to start of next day)
cross_day_length = prefix_length + suffix_length

# Answer is the maximum of within-day and cross-day rest
result = max(max_consecutive, cross_day_length)
print(result)
```

    else:
        break
